          "anchor": "stmt-cor-regular-maxcut",
          "exact_quote": "For every $\\eta>0$ there are an integer $p\\ge1$, deterministic normalized angles $\\gamma,\\beta\\in\\mathbb R^p$, and an integer $D_0\\ge2$ such that, for every integer $D\\ge D_0$ and every finite simple unweighted $(D+1)$-regular graph $G$ of girth greater than $2p+1$, $$\\label{eq:high-girth-maxcut}\n q_{p,G}(2\\gamma/\\sqrt D,\\beta)\n \\ge \\frac12+\\frac{P_*-\\eta}{\\sqrt D}.$$ The word $p,\\gamma,\\beta$ is independent of $D$, the graph size, and $G$; the displayed usual MaxCut cost angles, unlike the normalized angles $\\gamma$, are scaled with the degree.*\n\n\n*Let $G_{n,d}$ be uniform among simple labelled $d$-regular graphs on $n$ vertices, along admissible $n$ with $nd$ even. For the same word, put $$\\Delta_D(G)=\\operatorname{OPT}(G)\n             -q_{p,G}(2\\gamma/\\sqrt D,\\beta).$$ The word may be chosen so that $$\\label{eq:random-regular-maxcut}\n \\lim_{D\\to\\infty}\n \\limsup_{\\substack{n\\to\\infty\\\\ n(D+1)\\ \\mathrm{even}}}\n \\Pr_G\\!\\left[\\sqrt D\\,\\Delta_D(G_{n,D+1})>\\eta\\right]=0.$$ Here the inner limit keeps $D,p,\\gamma,\\beta$ fixed, and $\\Pr_G$ is only over the graph: $q_{p,G}$ already averages quantum measurement.",

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-fixed-word-transfer** [claim](#stmt-prop-tree-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-tree-transfer): Imported fixed-word identity with bipartition sign conjugation and normalization matching. -> Equality between SK value and limiting tree edge value.
- **step-root-shift-energy** [claim](#stmt-prop-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-energy): Vacuum deletion and exact edge decomposition, followed by occupation-controlled convergence. -> Root-shift energy identity and norm-continuity estimate.
- **step-weyl-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lem-weyl-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-weyl-bounds): Creation and annihilation estimates, displacement conjugation and graph-norm approximation. -> Smooth weighted bounds for ordinary and controlled Weyl displacements.
- **step-centered-sums** [claim](#stmt-lem-centered-sums) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-centered-sums): Exact occupation representation and convergence on fixed-particle smooth tensors. -> Smooth field limits of normalized child sums.
- **step-unitary-product-limit** [claim](#stmt-lem-unitary-clt) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-unitary-clt): Exponential-vector matrix elements, second-order correction, weighted bounds and finite differences. -> Smooth limiting unitary product, including its scalar correction and external parameter jets.
- **step-ordinary-updates** [claim](#stmt-lem-ordinary-updates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-ordinary-updates): Factor child circuits and induct over the fixed word using centered unitary products. -> Limiting root updates and equality of ordinary insertion energy with SK value.
- **step-commuting-substitution** [claim](#stmt-lem-commuting-substitution) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-commuting-substitution): Identify the limiting joint unitary group, then apply Fourier substitution with polynomial tail control. -> Smooth operator-valued substitution preserving adjoints and unitarity.
- **step-exclusion-gate-calculus** [claim](#stmt-thm-gate-calculus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-gate-calculus): Finite cut recursion, induction over signal depth and target influence distance, then continuity inside the sequence. -> Smooth maps, adjoints, insertions and conditional mask bounds for fixed gate sequences.
- **step-additive-responses** [claim](#stmt-cor-additive-response) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-additive-response): Introduce an external time in cut unitary ratios and differentiate their product limit. -> Centered field response plus scalar second response, with small-generator continuity.
- **step-rooted-gaussian-formula** [claim](#stmt-lem-gaussian-rooted-realization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-rooted-realization): Inductively normalize orthogonal child columns and extract the first Gaussian chaos. -> Exact scalar law and rooted energy coefficient identity.
- **step-bounded-trigonometric-density** [claim](#stmt-lem-classical-trigonometric-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-classical-trigonometric-density): Seed truncation, output-to-leaf Lipschitz approximation, cutoff and Fourier approximation with odd symmetrization. -> Bounded odd angle formula with bounded transmitted columns and trigonometric pointwise maps.
- **step-color-exclusions** [claim](#stmt-lem-classical-color-exclusion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-classical-color-exclusion): Unroll formula occurrences with ancestor color lists and combine disjoint-mask squared errors before applying grouped Lipschitz bounds. -> Finite orientation-free excluded formula with partition-uniform error control.
- **step-primary-classical-target** [claim](#stmt-prop-classical-near-optimum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-classical-near-optimum): Choose scalar stopping and mesh, smooth the sign output, then bound columns and impose type exclusions. -> Fixed classical synthesis target near the SK optimum.
- **step-fixed-field-estimates** [claim](#stmt-lem-fixed-field-exponentials) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-fixed-field-exponentials): Fourier decay of divided symbols and weighted Weyl bounds, including derivatives of moving field directions. -> Uniform smooth exponential and jet bounds without inverse Gram matrices.
- **step-orthogonal-quadratic-estimates** [claim](#stmt-lem-orthogonal-quadratics) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-orthogonal-quadratics): Normal ordering, orthogonal Hilbert–Schmidt kernel bounds and weight-commutator Gronwall estimates. -> Small centered quadratic generators and count-uniform exponential estimates.
- **step-helper-contraction** [claim](#stmt-prop-helper-contraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-orthogonal-quadratics-2): Separate diagonal and simple helper paths, retain lower cut responses, identify the actual relative unitary group and iterate after removing seed phases. -> Simultaneous insertion convergence off helpers and compressed-space root-map convergence up to seed phases.
- **step-classical-seeded-synthesis** [claim](#stmt-thm-classical-synthesis) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-orthogonal-quadratics-3): Compile pointwise operations and aggregations from outer ranks inward; approximate elementary masked controls only after contraction choices. -> Finite seeded circuit approximating the classical insertion and its rooted energy.
- **step-packet-estimates** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-packet-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-packet-estimates): Weyl identities, Taylor estimates and translated quadrature spectral cutoffs. -> Packet separation and probe center-preservation estimates.
- **step-high-directions** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-high-directions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-high-directions): Expand the exact initialized split and compare coherent packet centers and fixed occupation projections. -> Orthogonal external directions decoupling from bounded smooth families.
- **step-ensemble-reachability** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-ensemble-control) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-ensemble-control): Choose generic axes, interpolate finite squared projection pairs and prove finite-product reachability in the clock-extended group. -> Exact finite selective rotation lists with zero clocks.
- **step-selective-probe-errors** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-selective-accuracy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-selective-accuracy): Preserve centers first, estimate changing actual insertion directions, then cancel the first-order displacement using zero clocks. -> Uniform ordinary identity error and special rotation accuracy.
- **step-low-insertion-approximation** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-low-insertions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-low-insertions): Apply the forward approximation to vacuum and low insertion inputs and use exact unitarity. -> Smooth low approximants to actual insertion directions.
- **step-one-singular-echo** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-one-echo) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-one-echo): Compute the selective flip using actual directions, cancel the huge costs exactly and restore the special transport sign. -> Bounded seed-field update and preserved packet invariant.
- **step-gram-energy-identification** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-gram-induction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-gram-induction): Reduce finite Weyl sums to direction Grams and compute the root-vacuum one-particle energy pairing separately. -> Formal external-mode Gram law and rooted energy convergence at standard stages.
- **step-huge-transverse-projection** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse): Use spin reversal to force pairings between separated opposite ordinary packets. -> Vanishing transverse projections needed for huge-stage visibility.
- **step-remove-seeded-controls** [claim](#stmt-thm-seed-simulation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse-2): Choose small initialization, perturb target angles away from analytic zero sets, construct probe lists successively and finally choose the packet amplitude. -> One fixed finite ordinary word approximating the seeded limiting energy.
- **step-main-energy-assembly** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse-3): Allocate classical, helper, synthesis and seed-removal errors before invoking fixed-word transfer; use the upper bound and zero-angle monotonicity. -> Thermodynamic-first eventual Parisi optimality.
- **step-regular-maxcut-transfer** [claim](#stmt-cor-regular-maxcut) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rem-order-of-choices): Match MaxCut angles to negative tree cost, use the girth-local edge cone, and combine local random-regular convergence with imported optimum asymptotics. -> High-girth cut bound and separate random-regular successive-limit optimality comparison.
- **step-positive-temperature-curvature** [claim](#stmt-lem-positive-temperature-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-positive-temperature-bound): Differentiate self-consistency through Itô isometry, evaluate the endpoint through Cole–Hopf and compare free energy with the optimum. -> Curvature normalization, endpoint bound and integrated value lower bound.
- **step-finite-tree-message-limit** [claim](#stmt-lem-eams-finite-step) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-eams-finite-step): Induct on degree-dependent normalizers and moments; use conditional innovations, node-to-cavity comparisons and the exact edge expansion. -> Gaussian message law, output variance and corrected finite-step edge value.
- **step-positive-continuum-comparison** [claim](#stmt-lem-eams-positive-continuum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-eams-positive-continuum): Couple Euler innovations to Brownian increments; apply Gronwall, normalizer stability, Itô isometry and Riemann-sum estimates. -> Mesh error estimates for states, output and energy.
- **step-strong-classical-realization** [claim](#stmt-lem-classical-strong-limit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-classical-strong-limit): Prove characteristic-vector occupation convergence, extend by density and moment truncation, and estimate child occupation by independent replacements. -> Norm convergence and occupation-controlled rooted energy transfer.
- **step-independent-classical-target** [claim](#stmt-prop-classical-near-optimum-positive-temperature) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-classical-near-optimum-positive-temperature): Realize the finite-step vector, clip using diffusion coupling, then apply bounded trigonometric approximation and color exclusions. -> Independent classical synthesis target with the same exclusion interface.

There is a real odd finite descendant formula $F_N$ of height at most $N+1$ in the classical rooted space whose Gaussian coordinates have exactly the stated joint law and whose energy satisfies $$\\mathcal E(F_N)=\\sum_{j=0}^N A_j B_{j+1}.\n \\label{eq:scalar-rooted-energy}$$ The formula is bounded by one, although its transmitted columns need not be bounded.",

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-classical-trigonometric-density",
          "exact_quote": "Let $m^*=\\pi(z)$, where $z$ is a finite descendant formula with Gaussian leaf seeds. Assume that every transmitted child column is odd, and that each local map producing such a column or the output is a jointly odd smooth function of its local seed and finite list of odd aggregate inputs, with polynomial-growth derivatives. Purely local intermediate operations may be composed into these maps before the lemma is applied. For every $\\eta>0$, there is an odd bounded angle formula $\\theta$ such that $$\\lVert \\sin(2\\theta)-m^*\\rVert<\\eta.\n \\label{eq:angle-density}$$ Every sent column in the formula for $\\theta$ can be chosen bounded and odd. Its pointwise maps can be chosen to be finite real trigonometric sums in their finite lists of odd inputs, with odd joint parity for odd outputs. Its local seed inputs can be chosen bounded and odd. All these functions have bounded derivatives of every order in their continuous arguments.",
          "source_id": "PM-281.1"
        }

If $f$ is its original output and $f^{\\mathrm{col}}$ its colored output, set to zero on helper roots, then $$\\lVert f^{\\mathrm{col}}-f\\rVert^2\\le C(\\alpha+p_H),\n \\label{eq:color-exclusion-bound}$$ where $C$ depends only on the fixed color-free formula, not on the number or individual probabilities of its nonhelper colors.           "anchor": "stmt-lem-fixed-field-exponentials",
          "exact_quote": "Let $Q_j=q(d_j)$, $1\\le j\\le k$, commute, where $k$ is fixed and $\\lVert d_j\\rVert_a\\le C_a$ for every $a$. Let $F_s:\\mathbb R^k\\to\\mathbb R$ have polynomial bounds on every derivative, uniformly in the local seed $s$. For bounded $t$, the operators $\\exp(\\mathrm itF_s(Q))$, their adjoints, and their time derivatives are uniformly smooth.         {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-orthogonal-quadratics",
          "exact_quote": "Let $R_l$ be finitely many pairwise disjoint root masks, with $p_l=\\Pr(R_l)$. Suppose $d_l,e_l$ are supported on $R_l$ and are real-commuting, meaning that all fields $q(d_l),q(e_l)$ commute pairwise. Suppose also that for every $a$ $$\\lVert d_l\\rVert_a+\\lVert e_l\\rVert_a\\le C_a\\sqrt{p_l}.$$ Set $$Q=\\sum_l\\left(q(d_l)q(e_l)-\\langle d_l,e_l\\rangle\\right),\n \\qquad \\rho=\\left(\\sum_l p_l^2\\right)^{1/2}.$$ Then $Q=O_{\\mathrm{sm}}(\\rho)$. Its exponentials and adjoints are uniformly smooth for bounded times, independently of the number of columns. They converge to the identity strongly on smooth vectors as $\\rho\\to0$, and in every fixed lower smooth norm on families with all higher smooth norms bounded.",

        }

          "anchor": "stmt-prop-helper-contraction",
          "exact_quote": "Consider a fixed finite sequence in the parity-preserving, root-seed-commuting gate-calculus class, containing finitely many propagations <a href=\"#eq:desired-propagation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:desired-propagation\">[eq:desired-propagation]</a>, possibly with different $I,J,f,P$, and otherwise fixed gates. Replace every indicated propagation by <a href=\"#eq:helper-contraction-generator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:helper-contraction-generator\">[eq:helper-contraction-generator]</a>, and refine their sender partitions so that their largest part probability tends to zero. Then all insertion vectors off $K$ converge to those of the original sequence. The root maps and their adjoints have uniform conditional smooth bounds on the compressed space, and converge strongly there up to scalar phases depending only on the root label. The same estimates hold on cut subtrees. Responses through the fixed lower formulas for $f$ have the uniform jet estimates proved below; no convergence of arbitrary boundary jets in the growing list of partition-dependent top channels is asserted.",

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-seed-packet-estimates",
          "exact_quote": "The following estimates are uniform for bounded families of tails.*\n\n1.  *Displacements with bounded directions preserve all the seminorms $\\lVert \\cdot\\rVert_k$, with bounds depending only on the direction norm and $k$. For $\\lVert d\\rVert\\leq C$, $$W(rd)\\eta=\\eta+\\mathrm ir q(d)\\eta+O(r^2)\\lVert \\eta\\rVert_1\n     \\quad\\hbox{in norm}.$$ If $d_r-e_r\\to0$ in norm and both directions are bounded, then $(W(d_r)-W(e_r))\\eta_r\\to0$ in norm. These assertions also hold for $W_P$.*\n\n\n2.  *If $\\lVert d_r-e_r\\rVert\\geq\\kappa>0$, then for every integer $M$, $$\\label{eq:seed-packet-separation}\n     \\lvert \\langle W(d_r/r)\\eta_r,W(e_r/r)\\xi_r\\rangle\\rvert=O(r^M).$$ The conclusion is unchanged by inserting a bounded root spin matrix.*\n\n\n3.  *For bounded $d_r,v_r$ and fixed $\\tau$, conjugating the short probe $e^{-\\mathrm ir\\tau Pq(v_r)}$ through a packet gives $$\\label{eq:seed-packet-probe}\n     W(d_r/r)^*e^{-\\mathrm ir\\tau Pq(v_r)}W(d_r/r)\n     =e^{-\\mathrm i\\tau a_rP}e^{-\\mathrm ir\\tau Pq(v_r)},\n     \\qquad a_r=-2\\mathop{\\mathrm{Im}}\\langle v_r,d_r\\rangle.$$ Consequently a fixed finite list of short probes preserves its scaled centers and bounded centered-tail moments, without any smallness assumption on $a_r$.",
          "source_id": "PM-281.1"
        }
      ],

          "exact_quote": "Let $h_1,\\ldots,h_m$ be a finite list of nonzero vectors in $\\mathbb R^2$. There are two perpendicular axes $P_1,P_2$ in the $ZY$-plane with the following property. Given any $\\theta\\in\\mathbb R$, there is a finite list $(a_k,\\tau_k)$, with $a_k\\in\\{1,2\\}$, such that $$\\label{eq:seed-zero-clocks}\n \\sum_{k:a_k=a}\\tau_k=0\\quad(a=1,2),\n \\qquad\n \\prod_k e^{-\\mathrm i\\tau_k c_{j,a_k}P_{a_k}}\n =e^{-\\mathrm i\\theta X}\\quad(1\\leq j\\leq m).$$ The product is in chronological order, with later factors on the left.           "anchor": "stmt-lem-seed-selective-accuracy",
          "exact_quote": "Consider a stage whose vacuum state $u_r\\boldsymbol\\Omega$ has a finite packet expansion, with norm-$o(r)$ remainders and uniformly bounded scaled centers. <a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-thermodynamic-first-optimality**: At each desired accuracy, a finite ordinary QAOA word with deterministic angles independent of system size and disorder achieves the required limiting expected energy. The thermodynamic limit precedes increasing depth.
- **invariant-fixed-word-transfer**: The disorder-averaged thermodynamic SK energy of a fixed ordinary word equals its limiting scaled tree edge correlation. The word and angles remain fixed during both limits.
- **invariant-rooted-energy-convergence**: Strong occupation-embedded convergence together with uniformly bounded expected top-generation occupation identifies the scaled edge limit with the root-shift energy. Norm perturbations control the energy difference.
- **invariant-seeded-synthesis**: A finite seeded circuit approximates the ideal classical insertion off the helper masks. Its energy error includes the contribution from discarding helper output.
- **invariant-seed-removal**: Every fixed finite seeded word admits an ordinary cost–mixer approximation to its limiting tree energy at any prescribed tolerance. The approximation is selected before taking the thermodynamic limit.
- **invariant-maxcut-regimes**: A fixed normalized word gives the stated cut-fraction lower bound on sufficiently large-degree regular graphs satisfying the girth condition. The separate optimality comparison concerns random regular graphs in successive size and degree limits.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-use-interchanged-limits**: Increase depth with system size and claim that the theorem supplies a joint-limit guarantee. -> The construction fixes the complete ordinary word before the thermodynamic limit; its nested approximation choices do not justify exchanging limits.
- **anti-use-practical-angle-selection**: Treat asymptotic existence as an efficient procedure for choosing a hardware circuit and its angles. -> The approximation stages establish existence through successive limiting choices, without a uniform depth estimate or an efficient angle-selection contract.
- **anti-use-uncontrolled-occupation**: Infer the tree edge-energy limit from strong vector convergence alone. -> The edge decomposition has an excited-side remainder whose control requires the occupation hypothesis.
- **anti-use-feedback-controls**: Compile a neighbor aggregation whose descendant calculation can use the receiving type again. -> The calculation can feed back through its own parameter dependencies, invalidating the excluded cut recursion.
- **anti-use-unpinned-helper-contraction**: Apply helper contraction after the reserved helper has already been used by surrounding gates. -> The backtracking contraction relies on the helper polarization; an already affected helper does not satisfy the pinning premise.
- **anti-use-general-graph-optimality**: Use the regular MaxCut corollary to claim optimality for arbitrary graphs, all high-girth graphs, or fixed-degree instances. -> The deterministic lower bound requires regularity and the stated girth condition, while the optimality comparison is restricted to random regular graphs in successive limits.
- **anti-use-non-gaussian-or-external-field**: Transfer the SK convergence theorem directly to other coupling laws or an external longitudinal field. -> The supplied model uses independent standard real Gaussian pair interactions and zero field; the theorem supplies no such extension.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The engineering guarantee concerns limiting expected energies on an infinite-degree rooted space and a thermodynamic limit, followed by increasing circuit depth. Finite words exist at each accuracy, but their selection uses scalar diffusion inputs, approximation limits, helper refinement, generic angle perturbation, and selective-probe existence. These are not a finite decidable input–output contract with supplied computable choices. The source explicitly states: “No uniform depth estimate and no exchange of these limits is used.” Finite matrix or packet checks would verify subsidiary identities rather than certify the stated energy convergence or produce the required angles.

Suppose their occupation embeddings converge strongly to $v$ at a fixed finite height and their expected top-generation occupations are bounded uniformly in $D$. <a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-gaussian-zero-field-model** (hypothesis): The energy-convergence theorem concerns the Gaussian zero-field SK model with the stated unordered-pair normalization. Extensions to other coupling laws or external fields are ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Claiming the same optimality theorem for non-Gaussian couplings or a longitudinal external field.']
- **limit-fixed-word-order-of-limits** (hypothesis): The complete finite word and deterministic angles must be selected before the thermodynamic limit. The result does not justify exchanging the nested approximation limits or increasing the word length during fixed-word transfer. Blocked use: ['Claiming a joint size–depth convergence guarantee.', 'Applying fixed-word transfer to degree-dependent or disorder-adaptive angle choices.']
- **limit-no-effective-depth-or-angle-selection** (nonconstructive): The proof supplies finite-word existence for limiting expected energy without a quantitative finite-size estimate, required-depth bound, or efficient angle-selection procedure. Blocked use: ['Deriving an executable near-optimal angle-selection routine from the existence theorem.', 'Assigning a finite-device accuracy or circuit-resource budget from this proof.']
- **limit-expected-energy-versus-measurement** (formal_scope): The main theorem concerns disorder-averaged limiting expected energy. The MaxCut cut fraction already averages quantum measurement; the random-regular probability statement concerns graph randomness. Blocked use: ['Claiming that each measured spin configuration achieves the limiting optimum.', 'Interpreting graph-probability convergence as a measurement success-probability guarantee.']
- **limit-rooted-energy-occupation-premises** (hypothesis): Rooted edge-energy transfer requires centered, root-invariant, finite-radius vectors, strong occupation-embedded convergence at a fixed finite height, and uniformly bounded expected top-generation occupations. Blocked use: ['Identifying edge energy from strong vector convergence alone.', 'Applying the root-shift formula to vectors lacking the stated rooted symmetry or occupation control.']
- **limit-smooth-adjoint-convergence** (hypothesis): Smooth operator convergence includes convergence of adjoints and parameter jets on the common smooth domain. Bounds on adjoint jets do not establish their convergence. Blocked use: ['Passing adjoints or differentiated products through a limit using uniform bounds alone.']
- **limit-centered-normalized-child-sums** (hypothesis): The field limit for normalized child sums requires centering. The uncentered assertion uses child averages and yields a scalar vacuum mean instead. Blocked use: ['Applying the centered field formula to uncentered transmitted columns.', 'Discarding an uncentered normalized contribution in a cut calculation.']
- **limit-unitary-second-order-correction** (formal_scope): The small-unitary product limit retains an imaginary scalar correction from second derivatives. The first-order field alone does not determine the general product limit. Blocked use: ['Replacing a general small-unitary product by its first-order field exponential without checking the scalar correction.']
- **limit-strong-commuting-controls** (hypothesis): Commuting substitution requires strongly commuting self-adjoint signals, the stated polynomial and exponential convergence, and commutation of operator coefficients with signal spectral projections. Seed-dependent substitution also requires strong commutation with the local seed multiplication algebra. Blocked use: ['Substituting noncommuting signal operators into the joint functional-calculus formulas.', 'Treating seed dependence as an unrestricted operator coefficient.']
- **limit-exclusion-parity-and-control-algebra** (hypothesis): Exclusion gates require odd transmitted fields, smooth pointwise maps with the prescribed growth bounds, ancestor and target mask exclusions, and a common commuting control algebra preserving joint symmetry. Blocked use: ['Compiling feedback through a receiving or ancestor mask.', 'Using target spins as their own parameter controls.', 'Combining incompatible control axes inside the same excluded gate.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 281 (`lean/docs/281.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [QAOA attains the SK ground-state energy in the thermodynamic-first limit](../../preprints/QAOA-attains-the-SK-ground-state-energy-in-the-thermodynamic-first-limit-September-25-2026/QAOA-attains-the-SK-ground-state-energy-in-the-thermodynamic-first-limit-September-25-2026.pdf)
> - [Full support of the zero-temperature Sherrington–Kirkpatrick order parameter](../../preprints/Full-support-of-the-zero-temperature-Sherrington-Kirkpatrick-order-parameter-September-27-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The linked formalization supplies variational identities for the Gaussian zero-field Sherrington–Kirkpatrick ground-state energy used in the paper's QAOA argument. Conditional on an admissible Parisi minimizer and its associated diffusion, it proves convergence of the finite-system ground-state energy, identifies its limit with the Parisi value, and gives equivalent terminal-martingale and integrated-curvature formulas.
>
> It also proves convergence of finite Gaussian coefficient sums to the curvature integral, which approaches the ground-state value as the terminal time tends to one. The selected statement covers these value and approximation results; it does not itself assert convergence of QAOA circuit energies.
>
> The formalization proves that every admissible integrable minimizer of the zero-temperature Parisi functional for the pure zero-field Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model has full relative Stieltjes support on $[0,1)$. Equivalently, the order parameter increases strictly between every two overlap values below one, so its support has no gap. The covariance normalization is $\xi(t)=t^2/2$.
>
> The statement also constructs the associated diffusion and proves its selected self-consistency moment identities. It is conditional on the order parameter being a minimizer and does not separately assert existence of a minimizer.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

open MeasureTheory ProbabilityTheory Filter Set
open scoped Topology ENNReal NNReal BigOperators

namespace SKValue

noncomputable def standardGaussian : Measure ℝ := gaussianReal 0 1

instance : IsProbabilityMeasure standardGaussian := by unfold standardGaussian; infer_instance

noncomputable def gaussianProduct (ι : Type) [Fintype ι] : Measure (ι → ℝ) :=
  Measure.pi (fun _ : ι ↦ standardGaussian)
instance gaussianProduct_probability (ι : Type) [Fintype ι] :
    IsProbabilityMeasure (gaussianProduct ι) := by
  unfold gaussianProduct
  infer_instance

def coordinate (N : ℕ) (j : ℕ) (z : Fin (N+1) → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  z ⟨j % (N+1), Nat.mod_lt _ (Nat.succ_pos _)⟩

noncomputable def stepSize (T : ℝ) (N : ℕ) : ℝ := T / N
noncomputable def meshTime (T : ℝ) (N j : ℕ) : ℝ := j * stepSize T N

noncomputable def euler (T : ℝ) (N : ℕ) (γ : ℝ → ℝ) (u : ℝ → ℝ → ℝ)
    (z : Fin (N+1) → ℝ) : ℕ → ℝ
  | 0 => 0
  | j+1 => euler T N γ u z j + Real.sqrt (stepSize T N) * coordinate N j z +
      stepSize T N * γ (meshTime T N j) * u (meshTime T N j) (euler T N γ u z j)

structure OrderParameter where
  coeff : ℝ → ℝ
  nonneg : ∀ t ∈ Ico (0 : ℝ) 1, 0 ≤ coeff t
  monotone : MonotoneOn coeff (Ico (0 : ℝ) 1)
  rightContinuous : ∀ t ∈ Ico (0 : ℝ) 1, ContinuousWithinAt coeff (Ici t) t
  integrable : IntegrableOn coeff (Ioc (0 : ℝ) 1)

structure BrownianSpace where
  Ω : Type
  measurableSpace : MeasurableSpace Ω
  μ : @Measure Ω measurableSpace
  probability : @IsProbabilityMeasure Ω measurableSpace μ
  B : ℝ≥0 → Ω → ℝ
  measurable : ∀ t, @StronglyMeasurable Ω ℝ _ measurableSpace (B t)
  brownian : @IsBrownianReal Ω measurableSpace B μ

attribute [instance] BrownianSpace.measurableSpace BrownianSpace.probability

noncomputable def BrownianSpace.filtration (W : BrownianSpace) :
    Filtration ℝ≥0 W.measurableSpace := Filtration.natural W.B W.measurable

noncomputable def shiftedFiltration (W : BrownianSpace) (t : ℝ) :
    Filtration ℝ≥0 W.measurableSpace :=
  Filtration.natural (fun s ω ↦ W.B (Real.toNNReal t + s) ω - W.B (Real.toNNReal t) ω)
    (fun _ ↦ (W.measurable _).sub (W.measurable _))

def Admissible (W : BrownianSpace) (t : ℝ) (α : ℝ≥0 → W.Ω → ℝ) : Prop :=
  IsProgressive (shiftedFiltration W t) α ∧ ∀ s ω, |α s ω| ≤ 1

noncomputable def controlPayoff (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (t x : ℝ) (α : ℝ≥0 → W.Ω → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  ∫ ω, (|x + W.B 1 ω - W.B (Real.toNNReal t) ω +
    ∫ s in (0 : ℝ)..(1-t), γ.coeff (t+s) * α (Real.toNNReal s) ω| -
    (1/2 : ℝ) * ∫ s in (0 : ℝ)..(1-t), γ.coeff (t+s) * α (Real.toNNReal s) ω ^ 2) ∂W.μ

noncomputable def phi (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) (t x : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  sSup {r | ∃ α, Admissible W t α ∧ r = controlPayoff W γ t x α}

noncomputable def gradient (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) (t x : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  deriv (phi W γ t) x

noncomputable def curvature (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) (t x : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  deriv (gradient W γ t) x

noncomputable def parisi (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) : ℝ :=
  phi W γ 0 0 - (1/2 : ℝ) * ∫ t in (0 : ℝ)..1, t * γ.coeff t

def IsMinimizer (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) : Prop :=
  ∀ η : OrderParameter, parisi W γ ≤ parisi W η

def IsDiffusion (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) (X : ℝ → W.Ω → ℝ) : Prop :=
  Adapted W.filtration (fun t ω ↦ X (min (t : ℝ) 1) ω) ∧
  (∀ᵐ ω ∂W.μ, ContinuousOn (fun t ↦ X t ω) (Icc (0 : ℝ) 1) ∧
    ∀ t ∈ Icc (0 : ℝ) 1,
      X t ω = W.B (Real.toNNReal t) ω +
        ∫ s in (0 : ℝ)..t, γ.coeff s * gradient W γ s (X s ω))

noncomputable def spin (b : Bool) : ℝ := if b then 1 else -1

noncomputable def hamiltonian (n : ℕ) (J : (Fin n × Fin n) → ℝ)
    (σ : Fin n → Bool) : ℝ :=
  (Real.sqrt (n : ℝ))⁻¹ *
    ∑ i : Fin n, ∑ j : Fin n, if i < j then J (i,j) * spin (σ i) * spin (σ j) else 0

noncomputable def groundStateSequence (n : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  (n : ℝ)⁻¹ * ∫ J, sSup (range (hamiltonian n J)) ∂gaussianProduct (Fin n × Fin n)

noncomputable def groundStateValue : ℝ := limUnder atTop groundStateSequence

noncomputable def gradientStopped (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (X : ℝ → W.Ω → ℝ) (U : W.Ω → ℝ) (t : ℝ≥0) (ω : W.Ω) : ℝ :=
  if (t : ℝ) < 1 then gradient W γ t (X t ω) else U ω

def ScalarValueConclusion (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (X : ℝ → W.Ω → ℝ) : Prop :=
  Tendsto groundStateSequence atTop (𝓝 groundStateValue) ∧
  ∃ U : W.Ω → ℝ,
    Martingale (gradientStopped W γ X U) W.filtration W.μ ∧
    (∀ᵐ ω ∂W.μ, |U ω| ≤ 1 ∧ (U ω)^2 = 1 ∧
      Tendsto (fun t ↦ gradient W γ t (X t ω)) (𝓝[<] (1 : ℝ)) (𝓝 (U ω))) ∧
    Tendsto (fun t ↦ ∫ ω, (gradient W γ t (X t ω) - U ω)^2 ∂W.μ)
      (𝓝[<] (1 : ℝ)) (𝓝 (0 : ℝ)) ∧
    groundStateValue = (∫ ω, |X 1 ω| ∂W.μ) - ∫ t in (0 : ℝ)..1, t * γ.coeff t ∧
    groundStateValue = ∫ ω, U ω * W.B 1 ω ∂W.μ ∧
    groundStateValue = ∫ t in (0 : ℝ)..1, ∫ ω, curvature W γ t (X t ω) ∂W.μ ∧
    IntervalIntegrable (fun t ↦ ∫ ω, curvature W γ t (X t ω) ∂W.μ) volume 0 1

noncomputable def eulerPath (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) (T : ℝ) (N : ℕ)
    (z : Fin (N+1) → ℝ) (j : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  euler T N γ.coeff (gradient W γ) z j

noncomputable def rawCoefficient (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (T : ℝ) (N j : ℕ) (z : Fin (N+1) → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  curvature W γ (meshTime T N j) (eulerPath W γ T N z j)

noncomputable def normalizer (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (T : ℝ) (N j : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  (Real.sqrt (∫ z, (rawCoefficient W γ T N j z)^2 ∂gaussianProduct (Fin (N+1))))⁻¹

noncomputable def gaussianIncrement (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (T : ℝ) (N j : ℕ) (z : Fin (N+1) → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  normalizer W γ T N j * rawCoefficient W γ T N j z * coordinate N j z

noncomputable def roundedSign (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (T : ℝ) (N : ℕ) (z : Fin (N+1) → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  Real.sign (gradient W γ T (eulerPath W γ T N z N) +
    2 * cdf standardGaussian (coordinate N N z) - 1)

noncomputable def coefficientA (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (T : ℝ) (N j : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  if j = 0 then 0 else
    ∫ z, roundedSign W γ T N z * gaussianIncrement W γ T N (j-1) z
      ∂gaussianProduct (Fin (N+1))

noncomputable def coefficientB (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (T : ℝ) (N i : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  ∫ z, roundedSign W γ T N z * coordinate N (i-1) z ∂gaussianProduct (Fin (N+1))

noncomputable def shiftedCoefficientSum (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (T : ℝ) (N : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  ∑ j ∈ Finset.range (N+1), coefficientA W γ T N j * coefficientB W γ T N (j+1)

def FiniteGaussianConclusion (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter)
    (X : ℝ → W.Ω → ℝ) (T : ℝ) : Prop :=
  (∀ᶠ N in atTop, ∀ j < N, 0 < normalizer W γ T N j) ∧
  Tendsto (shiftedCoefficientSum W γ T) atTop
    (𝓝 (∫ t in (0 : ℝ)..T, ∫ ω, curvature W γ t (X t ω) ∂W.μ)) ∧
  Tendsto (fun S ↦ ∫ t in (0 : ℝ)..S, ∫ ω, curvature W γ t (X t ω) ∂W.μ)
    (𝓝[<] (1 : ℝ)) (𝓝 groundStateValue)

theorem value_consequences :
    (∀ (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) (X : ℝ → W.Ω → ℝ),
      IsMinimizer W γ → IsDiffusion W γ X → ScalarValueConclusion W γ X) ∧
    (∀ (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter) (X : ℝ → W.Ω → ℝ) (T : ℝ),
      0 < T → T < 1 → IsMinimizer W γ → IsDiffusion W γ X →
      FiniteGaussianConclusion W γ X T) ∧
    (∀ (W : BrownianSpace) (γ : OrderParameter),
      IsMinimizer W γ → groundStateValue = parisi W γ) := by
  sorry

end SKValue

end OAI
```

Conditional on an admissible Parisi minimizer and its associated diffusion, it proves convergence of the finite-system ground-state energy, identifies its limit with the Parisi value, and gives equivalent terminal-martingale and integrated-curvature formulas.\n\nIt also proves convergence of finite Gaussian coefficient sums to the curvature integral, which approaches the ground-state value as the terminal time tends to one. The selected statement covers these value and approximation results; it does not itself assert convergence of QAOA circuit energies.\n\nThe formalization proves that every admissible integrable minimizer of the zero-temperature Parisi functional for the pure zero-field Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model has full relative Stieltjes support on $[0,1)$. Equivalently, the order parameter increases strictly between every two overlap values below one, so its support has no gap. The covariance normalization is $\\xi(t)=t^2/2$.\n\nThe statement also constructs the associated diffusion and proves its selected self-consistency moment identities. It is conditional on the order parameter being a minimizer and does not separately assert existence of a minimizer."

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) approaches the ground-state energy per spin of the Gaussian zero-field Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model when system size tends to infinity first and circuit depth then increases. For every accuracy, some finite depth and deterministic angles, independent of system size and disorder, achieve that accuracy in the limiting expected energy per spin using the standard cost Hamiltonian and transverse-field mixer. This proves the eventual Parisi-optimality conjecture of Basso, Farhi, Marwaha, Villalonga, and Zhou in its fixed-parameter thermodynamic formulation. We give no quantitative bound on the required depth or efficient angle-selection procedure.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction and statement of the result

The Sherrington–Kirkpatrick (SK) model was introduced by Sherrington and Kirkpatrick <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[SK1975](#ref-SK1975)] as a mean-field Ising spin glass with independent Gaussian pair interactions. Parisi’s replica-symmetry-breaking description uses a hierarchy of order parameters <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Parisi1979](#ref-Parisi1979)]. Guerra’s interpolation established the Parisi upper bound for the pressure <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Guerra2003](#ref-Guerra2003)], and Talagrand proved equality <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Talagrand2006](#ref-Talagrand2006)].

Farhi, Goldstone, and Gutmann introduced the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) as alternating evolution under an objective Hamiltonian and a transverse-field mixer, starting from the uniform superposition <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[FGG2014](#ref-FGG2014)]. For this model, Farhi, Goldstone, Gutmann, and Zhou developed an exact description of its performance at every fixed depth in the infinite-size limit and proved concentration at fixed parameters <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[FGGZ2022](#ref-FGGZ2022)]. Basso, Farhi, Marwaha, Villalonga, and Zhou related that limit to QAOA on large-degree trees and conjectured that increasing depth eventually attains the Parisi optimum <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BFMVZ2022](#ref-BFMVZ2022), Section 7, Equation (7.2)]. Boulebnane et al. <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BKL2026](#ref-BKL2026)] proved a fixed-depth spin–boson representation and used it to obtain numerical evidence for convergence towards the Parisi value. Their representation provides a useful Hilbert-space language for the limit. The construction here must also realize bounded classical computations and eliminate longitudinal seed controls before selecting one ordinary word.

Sels and Morone <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[SM2026](#ref-SM2026)] studied QAOA on the SK model using a semiclassical truncated-Wigner approximation and compared its performance with quantum data. Their numerical results and heuristic analysis give further evidence for convergence to the Parisi value and suggest a depth scaling.

The order of limits distinguishes this question from the fixed-instance optimality proved by Farhi, Goldstone, and Gutmann <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[FGG2014](#ref-FGG2014), Section VI, Equation (10)]: for each fixed instance, increasing the depth approaches its optimum. That result does not provide a finite depth that works as the number of spins tends to infinity. We instead choose a complete finite word for each desired accuracy and then let the number of spins tend to infinity. The quantum construction simulates a finite classical calculation, using temporary Gaussian seed controls and then removing them.

There are two ways to supply the classical calculation. Auffinger, Chen, and Zeng proved that the zero-temperature Parisi order parameter has infinitely many support points <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ACZ2020](#ref-ACZ2020), Theorem 1]; Chen proved full support on $[0,1)$ and a smooth density <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Chen2026](#ref-Chen2026), Theorem 1.3]. Our primary route uses the companion *Full support of the zero-temperature Sherrington–Kirkpatrick order parameter <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[SKSupport2026](#ref-SKSupport2026)]* . Its scalar diffusion yields a finite function of independent Gaussian variables and a coefficient formula for its value. We realize that function directly in the rooted Hilbert space and identify the coefficient formula with its energy. The imported scalar facts are full support of the zero-temperature order parameter, the diffusion’s consistency and value identities, and a finite Gaussian coefficient identity. Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a> states these inputs before constructing their rooted realization.

We also give an independent positive-temperature route. It follows the Parisi-diffusion approach of Montanari <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Montanari2019](#ref-Montanari2019)], whose optimization theorem assumes interval support of the positive-temperature Parisi measure, and the broader framework of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[AMS2020](#ref-AMS2020)]. Lopatto’s full-support theorem <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Lopatto2026](#ref-Lopatto2026)] supplies that positive-temperature support property. The finite-iteration Gaussian and edge-energy analysis of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023)] then gives a near-optimal descendant formula. This second route has its own quantitative and finite-degree statements and does not use the zero-temperature companion.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Model and quantifiers

<a id="eq:sk-model"></a>
<a id="eq:parisi-constant"></a>
Let $(J_{ij})_{1\le i<j\le n}$ be independent standard real Gaussian random variables. For $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^n$, set $$\label{eq:sk-model}
 h_{n,J}(\sigma)=\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_{i<j}J_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j,
 \qquad
 C_{n,J}=\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_{i<j}J_{ij}Z_iZ_j,
 \qquad B_n=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i.$$ Here $X,Y,Z$ are the Pauli matrices. The positive SK optimum is $$\label{eq:parisi-constant}
 P_*=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\mathbb E\max_{\sigma}h_{n,J}(\sigma).$$ The ground-state Parisi formula and its attainment are due to Auffinger and Chen <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[AC2017](#ref-AC2017)]. We use the covariance convention $\xi(t)=t^2/2$; its exact relation to the unordered-pair model is stated with the scalar input in Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a>. Appendix <a href="#sec:positive-temperature-alternative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:positive-temperature-alternative">8</a> also obtains this limit from the positive-temperature formula and a log-sum comparison.

<a id="eq:qaoa-state"></a>
For $p\ge1$ and $\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb R^p$, define $$\label{eq:qaoa-state}
 \lvert \psi_{n,p,J}(\gamma,\beta)\rangle
 =e^{-\mathrm i\beta_pB_n}e^{-\mathrm i\gamma_pC_{n,J}}
 \cdots e^{-\mathrm i\beta_1B_n}e^{-\mathrm i\gamma_1C_{n,J}}
 \lvert +\rangle^{\otimes n}.$$ All parameters are deterministic. Write <a id="eq:qaoa-value"></a>$$\label{eq:qaoa-value}
 v_p(\gamma,\beta)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n
 \mathbb E\langle \psi_{n,p,J}(\gamma,\beta)\rvert C_{n,J}
 \lvert \psi_{n,p,J}(\gamma,\beta)\rangle,
 \qquad Q_p=\sup_{\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb R^p}v_p(\gamma,\beta).$$ The fixed-parameter limit exists <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[FGGZ2022](#ref-FGGZ2022), [BFMVZ2022](#ref-BFMVZ2022)].

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For the zero-field SK model <a href="#eq:sk-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sk-model">[eq:sk-model]</a>, <a id="eq:main-result"></a>$$\label{eq:main-result}
 \lim_{p\to\infty}Q_p=P_*.$$ Equivalently, for every $\varepsilon>0$ there exist a finite integer $p$ and deterministic real vectors $\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb R^p$, independent of $n$ and $J$, such that $v_p(\gamma,\beta)\ge P_*-\varepsilon$.*

</div>

This proves the conjectured eventual Parisi optimality in the fixed-parameter thermodynamic formulation of <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[BFMVZ2022](#ref-BFMVZ2022)]. Measurement in the computational basis immediately gives $Q_p\le P_*$. Appending a zero-angle layer gives $Q_{p+1}\ge Q_p$. Thus the theorem reduces to constructing a finite word at every prescribed accuracy. The final word may contain inverse gates, zero angles, and very large angles, all of which are allowed in <a href="#eq:qaoa-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:qaoa-state">[eq:qaoa-state]</a>. A finite word in costs and mixers can always be written in that alternating form by merging consecutive gates of the same kind and inserting identity gates.

The theorem is stated in expected energy. The argument gives no quantitative finite-size estimate, depth bound, or efficient procedure for choosing the angles.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### How the construction works

The proof separates a classical choice of target from its quantum implementation. The intermediate setting is the large-degree regular tree: each vertex carries an independent Gaussian label, and a finite rooted calculation only reads labels within a fixed radius. Normalized sums of centered child calculations become jointly Gaussian coordinates. Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a> gives a Hilbert-space realization of these coordinates and a continuous quadratic expression for the limiting energy.

The scalar companion provides a bounded odd function of finitely many independent Gaussians whose value approaches $P_*$. In Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a>, each new Gaussian is the normalized aggregate of a suitable calculation one level below. An orthogonality induction makes the joint Gaussian law exact. The one-particle part of the resulting function gives its rooted energy, so the scalar value formula passes to this realization without a separate finite-degree edge expansion.

We next approximate all transmitted functions by bounded smooth ones. A finite label assigns a *type* to each vertex. Splitting the label space into sufficiently small types lets every aggregation omit the receiver’s type, the types of all its path ancestors, and a small reserved set of helper types, at arbitrarily small energy cost. These exclusions prevent a compiled operation from feeding back into its own parameters. They do not assert independence of calculations using the same descendant subtree. Proposition <a href="#prop:classical-near-optimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:classical-near-optimum">21</a> gives the resulting bounded finite formula, with its output written as $\sin(2\theta)$ for a bounded angle calculation $\theta$.

Theorem <a href="#thm:classical-synthesis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classical-synthesis">22</a> implements these angles using cost and mixer gates together with a longitudinal field formed from the Gaussian labels. To implement a neighbor sum, commutators combine interactions along a receiver–sender–helper–sender walk. When the walk returns to the same sender, the helper spin, held in its initial state, supplies the desired term. We subdivide the sender type and retain only walks whose two sender visits lie in the same part. This keeps every returning walk, while the different-sender contribution vanishes as the parts become small. The proof also controls the effects inside the lower calculations, which are called repeatedly within a fixed surrounding circuit. Helpers have arbitrarily small total probability; discarding their output is included in the energy estimate.

Theorem <a href="#thm:seed-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:seed-simulation">26</a> then removes the longitudinal field from the already selected finite circuit. An imperfect cost echo creates a small component with a large coherent displacement. Selective spin rotations change that component while canceling their leading action on the rest of the state; a long cost echo magnifies the change into a finite longitudinal field. Separating its direction from the circuit’s existing Gaussian coordinates gives a seed that can be reused at every requested seed gate. A separate one-particle calculation shows that this Gaussian identification also preserves the rooted energy. The elementary ensemble-control ideas are related to those of Li and Khaneja <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[LiKhaneja2005](#ref-LiKhaneja2005)]; Section <a href="#sec:seed-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:seed-simulation">5</a> proves the estimates for one fixed circuit, including its changing insertion directions and the singular echo.

After all these choices there is one finite ordinary cost–mixer word. Only at this point do we invoke the fixed-word tree-to-SK identity of Basso et al. <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[BFMVZ2022](#ref-BFMVZ2022)]. The exact error allocation and the order of choices are given in Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a>, after the three constructions have been established.

<a id="sec:regular-maxcut"></a>
#### A regular MaxCut consequence

For a finite simple graph $G=(V,E)$ with $E\ne\varnothing$, put $$H_{\mathrm{MC}}(G)=\frac12\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}(I-Z_uZ_v),
 \qquad B_G=\sum_{v\in V}X_v.$$ For $a,b\in\mathbb R^p$, let $\langle\cdot\rangle^G_{p,a,b}$ denote expectation with the gate order and signs of <a href="#eq:qaoa-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:qaoa-state">[eq:qaoa-state]</a>, using cost $H_{\mathrm{MC}}(G)$, mixer $B_G$, angles $a,b$, and initial state $\lvert +\rangle^{\otimes |V|}$. The expected fraction of edges cut by a computational-basis measurement is $$q_{p,G}(a,b)=\frac{\langle H_{\mathrm{MC}}(G)\rangle^G_{p,a,b}}{|E|}.$$ Write $\operatorname{OPT}(G)=\operatorname{MaxCut}(G)/|E|$ for the maximum cut fraction.

<a id="stmt-cor-regular-maxcut"></a>
<div id="cor:regular-maxcut" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:high-girth-maxcut"></a>
**Corollary 2** (Regular MaxCut in successive limits). *For every $\eta>0$ there are an integer $p\ge1$, deterministic normalized angles $\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb R^p$, and an integer $D_0\ge2$ such that, for every integer $D\ge D_0$ and every finite simple unweighted $(D+1)$-regular graph $G$ of girth greater than $2p+1$, $$\label{eq:high-girth-maxcut}
 q_{p,G}(2\gamma/\sqrt D,\beta)
 \ge \frac12+\frac{P_*-\eta}{\sqrt D}.$$ The word $p,\gamma,\beta$ is independent of $D$, the graph size, and $G$; the displayed usual MaxCut cost angles, unlike the normalized angles $\gamma$, are scaled with the degree.*

<a id="eq:random-regular-maxcut"></a>
*Let $G_{n,d}$ be uniform among simple labelled $d$-regular graphs on $n$ vertices, along admissible $n$ with $nd$ even. For the same word, put $$\Delta_D(G)=\operatorname{OPT}(G)
             -q_{p,G}(2\gamma/\sqrt D,\beta).$$ The word may be chosen so that $$\label{eq:random-regular-maxcut}
 \lim_{D\to\infty}
 \limsup_{\substack{n\to\infty\\ n(D+1)\ \mathrm{even}}}
 \Pr_G\!\left[\sqrt D\,\Delta_D(G_{n,D+1})>\eta\right]=0.$$ Here the inner limit keeps $D,p,\gamma,\beta$ fixed, and $\Pr_G$ is only over the graph: $q_{p,G}$ already averages quantum measurement.*

</div>

The complete proof is given in Section <a href="#sec:regular-maxcut-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:regular-maxcut-proof">7</a>, after the fixed-word tree interface and the SK construction.

The optimality comparison concerns random regular graphs in these successive limits, not all high-girth graphs or fixed-degree optima. No joint size–degree limit is asserted. Here $p$ counts QAOA rounds, not a degree-uniform two-qubit gate depth; the cut fraction above averages quantum measurement.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Reading paths and conventions

Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a> develops the fixed-word rooted calculus. Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a> imports the scalar theorems and constructs the bounded excluded formula. Sections <a href="#sec:classical-synthesis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-synthesis">4</a> and <a href="#sec:seed-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:seed-simulation">5</a> implement it and remove the seeds. Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a> assembles the ordinary word and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Section <a href="#sec:regular-maxcut-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:regular-maxcut-proof">7</a> then proves the regular-MaxCut consequence from this theorem and the fixed-word tree interface.

For the positive-temperature reading path, Appendix <a href="#sec:positive-temperature-alternative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:positive-temperature-alternative">8</a> replaces the scalar input and its rooted realization. It derives the same classical conclusion independently, using the shared bounded approximation and exclusion lemmas of Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a>. The quantum constructions and final assembly are the same for both paths.

Physical system size is $n$, tree degree is $D+1$, and ordinary circuit depth is $p$. The scalar mesh count in Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a> is $N$; top-generation occupation operators appearing in the operator estimates are identified by their operator context. Mixer angles are denoted by $\beta$, inverse temperature by $\beta_0$, and the scalar Parisi coefficient $\gamma(t)$ is distinct from the vector of cost angles. Inner products are antilinear in the first argument.

All constants may depend on a fixed finite formula or word. Uniformity in any approximation parameter is stated when required. Degree limits are taken with all words and their angles fixed; further approximation limits concern the resulting tree values.

<a id="sec:foundations"></a>
### The fixed-word tree calculus

This section constructs the common space in which we compare ordinary QAOA words and classical formulas. It has three tasks: identify the fixed-word SK value with a tree limit, express that limit as a bounded quadratic form, and justify the controlled operations used in synthesis. The same space will carry the finite Gaussian construction in Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a>.

We first isolate the transfer from an ordinary word to the tree. A word always means a finite word with fixed real coefficients; none of the statements below makes a uniform assertion in its length or its coefficients. The initial vector $\boldsymbol\Omega$ below is the plus product state on the finite light cone used to define the edge expectation.

<a id="stmt-prop-tree-transfer"></a>
<div id="prop:tree-transfer" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:tree-transfer"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Fixed-word transfer). *Let $W$ be an ordinary QAOA word, and apply the same word to the $(D+1)$-regular tree with cost $C_D=D^{-1/2}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}Z_iZ_j$, with each unordered edge counted once. Its expectation at an edge is defined by restricting to the finite light cone of that edge. Then $$\label{eq:tree-transfer}
 v_W=\lim_{D\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt D}{2}
       \langle W_D\boldsymbol\Omega\rvert Z_iZ_j\lvert W_D\boldsymbol\Omega\rangle.$$ Here $v_W$ is the disorder-averaged thermodynamic SK energy per vertex, with the normalization in the introduction. Both limits exist for every fixed word and every fixed choice of its angles.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-tree-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-tree-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* This is Theorem 1 of <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BFMVZ2022](#ref-BFMVZ2022)], together with its normalizations in Equations (2.3), (2.6), and (6.1)–(6.2). Its Equation (3.11) gives the fixed-depth tree value, and Equation (6.3) identifies that value with the SK limit. That paper uses the negative of our tree cost. Conjugation by $X$ on one bipartition of the tree changes the sign of every $Z_iZ_j$, preserves every mixer, and fixes the initial vector. It therefore changes the sign of the edge correlation as well, giving precisely <a href="#eq:tree-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-transfer">[eq:tree-transfer]</a>. The SK convention already agrees with the positive Gaussian Hamiltonian in its Equation (6.1). Consecutive gates of the same kind and inverses are included by inserting zero-angle gates. ◻

</details>

We first construct the rooted space and identify its energy functional. This also gives the Gaussian coordinates used by the classical construction. We then prove the weighted convergence estimates that realize ordinary words in this space and justify controlled gates inside a fixed surrounding circuit.

<a id="sec:pointed-spaces"></a>
#### Pointed spaces and occupation embeddings

The number of children will tend to infinity while the number of circuit layers remains fixed. We therefore organize a symmetric collection of child states by how many differ from the initial state. The limiting space is a symmetric Fock space: its $m$-particle sector records $m$ such child excitations, not $m$ physical spins. The bosonic representation is related to the fixed-depth spin–boson description of Boulebnane et al. <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[BKL2026](#ref-BKL2026)]; the occupation, insertion, and surrounding-circuit estimates used below are proved here.

Let $(S,\mu,\iota)$ be a probability space with a measure-preserving involution. The cases needed below are a one-point space and a standard Gaussian, with $\iota(s)=-s$. Extra independent marks may be included in $S$. Set $$\mathsf k=\mathbb C^2\otimes L^2(S,\mu),\qquad
 \omega=\lvert +\rangle\otimes\mathbf 1.$$ For any pointed space below, write $\langle T\rangle_0=
\langle \boldsymbol\Omega\rvert T\lvert \boldsymbol\Omega\rangle$ for the vacuum mean and $Q=1-|\boldsymbol\Omega\rangle\langle\boldsymbol\Omega|$ for its centered projection. All seed variables act by multiplication. In particular, a circuit never changes their values. The local symmetry is $X\otimes(f\mapsto f\circ\iota)$.

<a id="eq:recursive-spaces"></a>
For a pointed Hilbert space $(H,\boldsymbol\Omega)$ write $H^\circ=H\ominus\mathbb C\boldsymbol\Omega$ and $\Gamma_s(H^\circ)=\bigoplus_{m\geq0}(H^\circ)^{\otimes_s m}$. At finite height define $$\label{eq:recursive-spaces}
 \mathcal H_0=\mathsf k,\qquad
 \mathcal H_{h+1}=\mathsf k\otimes\Gamma_s(\mathcal H_h^\circ),\qquad
 \boldsymbol\Omega_{h+1}=\omega\otimes\Omega.$$ The embedding from height $h$ to height $h+1$ appends empty descendants: it is $\xi\mapsto\xi\otimes\Omega$ at height zero, and thereafter is obtained by applying the symmetric Fock functor to the preceding embedding. These embeddings preserve the vacuum. We identify the spaces with their images and use the completion of their union when convenient. Every construction in the proof takes place at some finite height before this completion is used.

Write $R_\iota f=f\circ\iota$. The joint spin/seed parity on these spaces is the vacuum-preserving involution $$\mathcal{P}_0=X\otimes R_\iota,\qquad
 \mathcal{P}_{h+1}=(X\otimes R_\iota)\otimes
       \Gamma_s(\mathcal{P}_h|_{\mathcal H_h^\circ}).$$ These involutions respect the height embeddings and fix the vacuum, so every odd observable has zero vacuum mean. We suppress the height in $\mathcal{P}$ when the space is understood.

The analogous invariant space on a rooted $D$-ary tree is $$\mathcal H_{D,0}=\mathsf k,\qquad
 \mathcal H_{D,h+1}=\mathsf k\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^D(\mathcal H_{D,h}).$$ The symmetry here permutes children together with their entire subtrees and seed coordinates. It does not discard the seeds. For any pointed space $H$, the orthogonal decomposition by the number of nonvacuum tensor factors gives an isometry <a id="eq:occupation-embedding"></a>$$\label{eq:occupation-embedding}
 \mathcal I_D:\operatorname{Sym}^D(H)\longrightarrow
       \bigoplus_{m=0}^D(H^\circ)^{\otimes_s m}.$$ On a symmetric $m$-particle tensor, the inverse distributes its $m$ nonvacuum factors over the $D$ slots with coefficient $\binom Dm^{-1/2}$. Applying this isometry recursively gives isometries $\mathcal I_{D,h}:\mathcal H_{D,h}\to\mathcal H_h$. Their range projections tend strongly to the identity at each fixed height. Indeed, a finite-particle vector involving finitely many child vectors is approximated inductively by vectors in these ranges. The finite-tree parity is the tensor product of the local involutions over its vertices; the occupation embeddings intertwine it with $\mathcal{P}_h$.

Creation is linear in its argument, annihilation is antilinear, and $$q(d)=a^*(d)+a(d),\qquad
 [q(d),q(e)]=2\mathrm i\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle d,e\rangle.$$ Conjugation by the recursive parity sends the child field $q(d)$ to $q(\mathcal{P}_h d)$; an odd child direction therefore gives an odd field at the next height. The fields are first defined on finite-particle vectors and then closed. Each $q(d)$ is essentially self-adjoint there, as follows either from the one-mode Schrödinger representation or from the convergence of its exponential series on finite-particle vectors. We use $\mathcal W(d)=e^{\mathrm iq(d)}$. In particular, <a id="eq:weyl-shift"></a>$$\label{eq:weyl-shift}
 \mathcal W(d)^*q(v)\mathcal W(d)
       =q(v)-2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v,d\rangle.$$

<a id="sec:root-energy"></a>
#### Root insertions and edge energy

At finite degree, a rooted vector records the response of a root observable to a circuit. Let $U$ be a circuit on a finite subtree and $U_j$ its restriction to child subtree $j$. Factoring the child operations defines the root interaction-picture unitary $u$ by $$U=\left(\bigotimes_{j\text{ child}}U_j\right)u.$$ For a root Pauli observable $P$, its insertion vector is $$f_P=U^*PU\boldsymbol\Omega=u^*Pu\boldsymbol\Omega,$$ because the child operations commute with $P$. For a word evaluated on a neighborhood containing both endpoint light cones, the pairing of its two $Z$ insertions gives the expectation of $Z_iZ_j$. We will prove convergence of ordinary insertion vectors after establishing the weighted estimates below. First we identify the energy of any convergent family of rooted vectors with bounded expected occupation; this geometric step needs no circuit estimates.

The insertion vector lives at one root, whereas an edge joins two rooted neighborhoods. The following operator moves one centered vector down to a single child while placing the new root in its initial state. It will turn the edge pairing into a quadratic form on one common space.

Define the centered root shift by <a id="eq:root-shift"></a>$$\label{eq:root-shift}
 \mathsf L:\mathcal H^\circ\longrightarrow\mathcal H^\circ,
 \qquad \mathsf Lv=\omega\otimes a^*(v)\Omega.$$ At height $h$ its range lies at height $h+1$. The definitions are compatible with the height embeddings, so $\mathsf L$ is an isometry on the centered direct limit. Extend it by $\mathsf L\boldsymbol\Omega=0$ when writing $\mathsf L^*$; this extension is a partial isometry on the whole pointed space. In particular $\mathsf L^*$ extracts the root-vacuum one-particle component, with a constant root seed.

<a id="stmt-prop-energy"></a>
<div id="prop:energy" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:energy-shift"></a>
<a id="eq:energy-continuity"></a>
**Proposition 4** (Energy as a root shift). *Let $f_D$ be centered, root-invariant, finite-radius vectors on the $(D+1)$-regular tree. Root invariance means that the vector itself is fixed by every rooted permutation of complete child subtrees, including their seed coordinates, at every vertex of its finite neighborhood. Suppose their occupation embeddings converge strongly to $v$ at a fixed finite height and their expected top-generation occupations are bounded uniformly in $D$. Place the same rooted vector at the endpoints of an edge, calling the resulting vectors $f_{D,i}$ and $f_{D,j}$. Then $$\label{eq:energy-shift}
 \lim_{D\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt D}{2}
       \langle f_{D,i},f_{D,j}\rangle
       =\mathcal E(v):=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\langle v,\mathsf Lv\rangle.$$ For any centered $v,w$, $$\label{eq:energy-continuity}
 |\mathcal E(v)-\mathcal E(w)|
       \leq(\|v\|+\|w\|)\|v-w\|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-energy</summary>

<a id="eq:vacuum-deletion"></a>
*Proof.* We give the exact finite-degree decomposition. Put $M=D+1$, let $E$ be the pointed child space, and write $N_M$ for the number of nonvacuum children in $\mathsf k\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^M(E)$. Let $R_M$ contract a distinguished child against its vacuum, and let $$J_M\xi=\omega\otimes\frac1{\sqrt M}
 \sum_{j=1}^M\boldsymbol\Omega^{\otimes(j-1)}\otimes\xi
                         \otimes\boldsymbol\Omega^{\otimes(M-j)}.$$ The latter is an isometry from $E^\circ$. Under occupation embeddings, $J_M^*=\mathsf L^*$ and $$\label{eq:vacuum-deletion}
 R_M=\sqrt{1-N/M},\qquad
 \|R_Mf-f\|^2\leq M^{-1}\langle f\rvert N_M\lvert f\rangle.$$ Indeed, in the $m$-particle sector the fraction of placements surviving deletion is $\binom{M-1}m/\binom Mm=1-m/M$.

<a id="eq:exact-edge-decomposition"></a>
Delete the distinguished edge and let $P_A,P_B$ denote the vacuum projections on its two half-trees. Decompose $f_{D,i}$ with $P_B+(1-P_B)$ and $f_{D,j}$ with $P_A+(1-P_A)$. The term with both vacuum projections is zero: the surviving half-tree vectors are centered. If just one side is excited, the other side being vacuum forces the root and all the other branches of the first vector to be vacuum. With $s_D=J_M^*f_D$ and $r_D=R_Mf_D$, the two such terms are $\langle s_D,r_D\rangle/\sqrt M$ and its conjugate. Heights in this pairing are matched by adding empty descendants. Each remaining excited-side vector has squared norm $\langle f_D\rvert N_M\lvert f_D\rangle/M$, by symmetry among branches. Thus $$\label{eq:exact-edge-decomposition}
 \langle f_{D,i},f_{D,j}\rangle
 =\frac2{\sqrt M}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\langle s_D,r_D\rangle+\varepsilon_D,
 \qquad
 |\varepsilon_D|\leq\frac{\langle f_D\rvert N_M\lvert f_D\rangle}M.$$ After multiplication by $\sqrt D/2$ the error tends to zero. Equation <a href="#eq:vacuum-deletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vacuum-deletion">[eq:vacuum-deletion]</a> and strong convergence show that the first term tends to $\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\langle \mathsf L^*v,v\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\langle v,\mathsf Lv\rangle$. This proves <a href="#eq:energy-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-shift">[eq:energy-shift]</a>.

Finally, <a href="#eq:energy-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-continuity">[eq:energy-continuity]</a> follows by adding and subtracting $\langle w,\mathsf Lv\rangle$ and using $\|\mathsf L\|=1$. ◻

</details>

The occupation hypothesis in Proposition <a href="#prop:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:energy">4</a> is a sufficient hypothesis for the displayed proof; strong convergence alone does not bound the remainder in <a href="#eq:exact-edge-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-edge-decomposition">[eq:exact-edge-decomposition]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:ordinary-updates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ordinary-updates">11</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:gate-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:gate-calculus">14</a> will supply that hypothesis for the quantum gates used here.

<a id="stmt-rem-classical-rotation-energy"></a>
<div id="rem:classical-rotation-energy" class="remark">

*Remark 5* (Classical rotations). The classical sector consists of vectors whose spin is $\lvert +\rangle$ at every vertex and whose coefficients depend only on seeds. If a $Y$ rotation whose angle depends only on seeds produces an insertion $v=\lvert +\rangle m+\lvert -\rangle n$ with seed-only coefficients in all descendants, then $\mathcal E(v)=\mathcal E(\lvert +\rangle m)$. Indeed every $\mathsf Lv$ has root spin $\lvert +\rangle$, while $\mathsf L(\lvert -\rangle n)$ has a child spin excitation and is orthogonal to the classical sector. Seed-only here permits dependence on descendant seeds; it does not mean dependence only on the root seed.

</div>

<a id="subsec:classical-gaussian-sector"></a>
#### Classical Gaussian positions and descendant formulas

The root-shift functional also applies to classical vectors, before they are compiled into quantum gates. We now give a common Gaussian realization for both classical constructions in this paper. This is a definition on the limiting pointed space; a finite-degree energy limit still requires the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:energy">4</a>.

Keep every quantum spin in $\lvert +\rangle$ and use real coefficients in the seed and symmetric Fock factors. This gives the real classical subspace. For a real centered classical child vector $f$, let $\mathsf G(f)=q(f)$ be the position operator on the root’s child Fock factor. For any finite list $f_1,\ldots,f_k$, these positions strongly commute and have joint centered Gaussian vacuum law with covariance $\langle f_i,f_j\rangle$. Indeed, choose a real orthonormal basis of their finite-dimensional span. The position operators of these orthogonal modes act on separate Fock factors, and $$\left\langle
       e^{\mathrm i\sum_{j=1}^k t_j\mathsf G(f_j)}
 \right\rangle_0
 =\exp\left(-\frac12\left\|\sum_{j=1}^kt_jf_j\right\|^2\right).$$ In particular, orthonormal directions give independent standard normal positions: the variance convention is $\|f\|^2$, with no additional factor of two. The positions act trivially on the local seed factor. Their joint vacuum law is therefore independent of the receiving root’s seed, even when a child direction itself depends on the seed at its own root.

<a id="eq:classical-gaussian-isometry"></a>
We identify a scalar function of these commuting variables with the vector obtained by applying that function to the vacuum. Under this identification, $$\lVert \bigl(\mathsf G(f)-\mathsf G(g)\bigr)\boldsymbol\Omega\rVert
      =\lVert f-g\rVert.
 \label{eq:classical-gaussian-isometry}$$ All fields are realized on the same pointed space, so this equality also specifies their coupling. The empty-descendant embeddings in <a href="#eq:recursive-spaces" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recursive-spaces">[eq:recursive-spaces]</a> preserve inner products, commute with passing to the corresponding Fock fields, and keep the local seed factor fixed. Thus directions constructed at different finite heights can first be embedded in one common height without changing their joint Gaussian law.

<a id="stmt-def-finite-descendant-formula"></a>
<div id="def:finite-descendant-formula" class="definition">

**Definition 6** (Finite descendant formula). A finite descendant formula is a finite expression built recursively from a local seed and finitely many Gaussian positions of centered formulas one level below, using pointwise scalar functions. The functions are required to be measurable and to produce square-integrable vectors whenever the formula is used as a vector. Additional empty levels identify formulas of different radii. Its finite-degree version replaces every Gaussian position by the normalized sum of the corresponding independent child variables.

</div>

The definition by itself is a limiting-space construction, not a finite-degree convergence theorem. Later approximation arguments impose boundedness or polynomial-growth conditions on the pointwise maps as needed. Because $\mathsf L$ places a constant seed at the new root, $\mathsf L^*$ extracts precisely the first Gaussian chaos with constant receiving-root seed. If $e_1,\ldots,e_k$ are orthonormal real centered child vectors and $F$ is a square-integrable function of $\mathsf G(e_1),\ldots,\mathsf G(e_k)$ alone, this gives <a id="eq:classical-first-chaos"></a>$$\label{eq:classical-first-chaos}
 \mathsf L^*F
   =\sum_{j=1}^k
       \mathbb E\bigl[F\,\mathsf G(e_j)\bigr]e_j.$$ Here $F$ may have a nonzero constant component, which $\mathsf L^*$ annihilates. The identity follows by the orthogonal projection onto the first chaos in these Gaussian coordinates; the remaining orthogonal child modes and all higher chaoses have zero projection. This is the normalization used in the primary classical construction.

The rooted space, Gaussian positions, and energy functional now give the setting for the classical construction in Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a>. The rest of this section establishes convergence and continuity of the quantum operations that will implement it.

<a id="sec:smooth-topology"></a>
#### Weights and the topology of convergence

The fields just defined are unbounded. To pass their products and parameter derivatives through limits, we need a common domain and bounds that control both seed moments and excitations at every level. These recursive weights serve that purpose; they are distinct from the top-generation number weight used in the seed-removal argument. If all local-seed coefficients under consideration are uniformly bounded, take $W_0=1$ on $\mathsf k$. For coefficients of polynomial growth in a Gaussian seed, start with multiplication by $1+|s|$ and make it block diagonal at $\omega$: $$W_0=P_0+Q_0(1+|s|)Q_0,
 \qquad P_0=\lvert \omega\rangle\langle \omega\rvert,\quad Q_0=1-P_0.$$ Compression is understood on its natural closed domain. This is a positive self-adjoint operator bounded below by one. It differs from multiplication by $1+|s|$ by a bounded finite-rank operator whose range consists of vectors with all seed moments. Consequently their positive-integer power domains coincide, with equivalent graph norms. For completeness, this last assertion follows by induction from $A^m-B^m=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}A^j(A-B)B^{m-1-j}$ on the common core; every occurrence of $A-B$ has smooth finite-dimensional range, and the resulting estimates close in both directions.

Since $W_0\omega=\omega$, we can recursively set <a id="eq:recursive-weights"></a>$$\label{eq:recursive-weights}
 \Lambda_h=W_h|_{\mathcal H_h^\circ},\qquad
 W_{h+1}=W_0\otimes1+1\otimes\mathrm d\Gamma(\Lambda_h).$$ Thus $W_h\boldsymbol\Omega_h=\boldsymbol\Omega_h$ and $W_h\geq1$. On a finite tree use $W_{D,h+1}=W_0+\sum_{j=1}^D Q_jW_{D,h}^{(j)}Q_j$. The occupation embeddings intertwine these weights exactly. This also shows that their range projections commute with every power of $W_h$.

Write $$\mathcal{S}_h=\bigcap_{m\geq0}\operatorname{Dom}(W_h^m),
 \qquad \|\xi\|_m=\|W_h^m\xi\|.$$ Finite-particle tensors of smooth child vectors and local vectors with all seed moments are dense in every one of these graph norms. One proof first cuts off the spectral variables of $W_0$ and each $\Lambda_h$, then cuts off particle number, and finally approximates in the resulting bounded spectral subspaces by algebraic tensors. The finite-degree projections give simultaneous approximants in all fixed graph norms.

When comparing unitary maps on the varying embedded spaces, extend each embedded unitary by the identity on the orthogonal complement of its range projection. These are genuine unitaries on the common Hilbert space. The projections commute with the weights and converge strongly to one in every fixed graph norm on smooth vectors, so this convention preserves all the bounds and limits. Parameter derivatives of the extensions vanish on that complement.

<a id="eq:smooth-family-bound"></a>
We will repeatedly use the following precise convention. An operator family $F_D(x)$ has *uniform smooth bounds* if, for every nonnegative integer $m$ and multi-index $\alpha$, there are $r,k,C$, independent of $D$ and $x$, such that $$\label{eq:smooth-family-bound}
 \|W_D^m\partial_x^\alpha F_D(x)\xi\|
 \leq C(1+|x|)^k\|W_D^r\xi\|,
 \qquad \xi\in\mathcal{S}_D.$$ The same condition is required of the adjoint family. Constants may depend on the fixed expression, its finite list of channels, the height, $m$, and $\alpha$. Dependence on a further limiting parameter is explicitly stated when relevant. Smooth convergence of operator families means strong convergence on smooth inputs of both the families and their adjoints, including all specified parameter jets, with these uniform bounds. The limits of the adjoint jets must be the adjoints of the corresponding limiting jets on the common domain; bounded adjoint jets alone do not imply their convergence. For vector families only the vector convergence is required. This definition also permits triangular arrays of one-copy spaces under the embeddings above.

<a id="eq:weight-interpolation"></a>
Two elementary consequences will be useful. Products of a fixed number of such families have the same bounds, with a possibly larger loss of input powers. Also, strong convergence together with bounded higher graph norms gives convergence in every fixed lower graph norm. The latter follows from the spectral inequality $$\label{eq:weight-interpolation}
 \|W^m\xi\|\leq R^m\|\xi\|
       +R^{m-M}\|W^M\xi\|\qquad(M>m, R\geq1),$$ by taking the strong limit first and $R\to\infty$ second. Consequently it is enough to prove convergence on the dense domain just described, and then use the uniform estimates. This is the meaning of convergence between different finite-degree spaces.

<a id="stmt-lem-weyl-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:weyl-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:weyl-number-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:weyl-small-bound"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Weyl estimates). *Let $N$ be the particle number on $\Gamma_s(H^\circ)$. For every integer $m\geq0$ there is $C_m$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \|(1+N)^m\mathcal W(d)\xi\|
 &\leq C_m(1+\|d\|)^{2m}\|(1+N)^m\xi\|,
