Such axes exist: each prohibited equality or zero is a nontrivial trigonometric equation in the axis angle, apart from the stated equal-or-opposite exception. Delete duplicate square pairs; on each deleted pair the product $c_{j,1}c_{j,2}$ is the same.

In $G=SU(2)^m\times\mathbb R^2$, take the two generators having spin components $-\mathrm ic_{j,a}P_a$ and clock component the $a$-th coordinate vector. Their brackets have zero clocks. A first bracket has $\mathrm iX$-coefficient equal, up to a common nonzero real constant, to $c_{j,1}c_{j,2}$. Repeated double brackets with the first or second generator multiply this coefficient by $c_{j,1}^2$ or $c_{j,2}^2$, again up to nonzero common constants. Thus the generated Lie algebra contains the clock-zero vector with coefficients $$c_{j,1}c_{j,2}F(c_{j,1}^2,c_{j,2}^2)\,\mathrm iX$$ for every real polynomial $F$. Polynomial interpolation on the finite set of square pairs makes all these coefficients equal to one.

For completeness, exponentials of this Lie-algebra vector are reachable by finite products of the two original one-parameter groups. Let $\mathcal R$ denote the subgroup of such finite products, and let $\mathfrak g_0$ be the Lie algebra generated by the two generators. The span of their conjugates by elements of $\mathcal R$ is invariant under each of the two adjoint one-parameter groups. Differentiation shows that it contains all iterated brackets and hence equals $\mathfrak g_0$. Choose finitely many of these conjugates forming a basis. The product map of their one-parameter groups has surjective differential at zero, so its image contains a neighborhood of the identity in the connected analytic subgroup with Lie algebra $\mathfrak g_0$. Each factor is itself a reachable conjugate. Subdivide any one-parameter path into finitely many pieces in this neighborhood. The whole exponential is therefore in $\mathcal R$. Applying this to the common $X$-generator with zero clocks gives <a href="#eq:seed-zero-clocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-zero-clocks">[eq:seed-zero-clocks]</a>. ◻

</details>

The preceding lemma treats fixed real coefficients. In the circuit, the coefficients come from actual insertion vectors and change during the probes. The next estimate controls that change without replacing the insertion vectors by approximants before a singular rescaling.

<a id="stmt-lem-seed-selective-accuracy"></a>
<div id="lem:seed-selective-accuracy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Accuracy of selective probes). *Consider a stage whose vacuum state $u_r\boldsymbol\Omega$ has a finite packet expansion, with norm-$o(r)$ remainders and uniformly bounded scaled centers. Suppose the ordinary component has order-one amplitude and the special component has amplitude $O(r)$. All designated tails have bounded top-number moments. Suppose also that one of the following ordinary geometries holds.*

1.  *At a standard stage all ordinary centers are zero.*

2.  *At a huge-cost stage the ordinary part is $\psi_++\psi_-$, with $Z\psi_y=y\psi_y$ and centers $-tyv_r$, where $t\neq0$ and $v_r=u_r^*Zu_r\boldsymbol\Omega$. Every special center $d_{j,r}$ obeys $\lim\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v_r,d_{j,r}\rangle\neq0$.*

*Assume the limiting special projection pairs $$h_j=\lim_{r\downarrow0}-2
 \bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v_{Z,r},d_{j,r}\rangle,
       \mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v_{Y,r},d_{j,r}\rangle\bigr)$$ exist and are nonzero. Use the axes and finite pulse list of Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-ensemble-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-ensemble-control">29</a> for these pairs, replacing its spin generators by actual toggled probes of angles $r\tau_k$. Then the net probe action is the identity up to $o(r)$ on every ordinary packet, and is the prescribed $X$-rotation up to $o(1)$ on every normalized special packet. The ordinary assertion holds also after any root spin matrix has been inserted in its tail. These statements hold uniformly for bounded families of tails sharing the stated center list, with the probes chosen solely from the vacuum data.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-selective-accuracy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-selective-accuracy</summary>

*Proof.* Let $V_j$ be a prefix of the short probes, and write $v_{P,r}^{(j)}=(V_ju_r)^*P(V_ju_r)\boldsymbol\Omega$. Center preservation and all centered-tail bounds follow first from Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-packet-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-packet-estimates">27</a>, without estimating the spin phases. At a standard stage every ordinary probe has zero center phase.

<a id="eq:seed-huge-imaginary"></a>
<a id="eq:seed-huge-imaginary-modulus"></a>
At a huge-cost stage there is the exact identity <a id="eq:seed-hermitian-diagonal"></a>$$\label{eq:seed-hermitian-diagonal}
 \langle v_{P,r}^{(j)},v_r\rangle
 =\langle PV_j\psi,V_jZ\psi\rangle,\qquad \psi=u_r\boldsymbol\Omega.$$ Group the ordinary packets by their original sign $y$. The diagonal term of sign $y$ on the right-hand side is $y\langle PV_j\psi_y,V_j\psi_y\rangle$, which is real. This remains true although the probes may rotate the spin within that group. The two ordinary centers are separated by $2|t|$, since $\lVert v_r\rVert=1$. An ordinary center and a special center are also separated: their imaginary projections against $v_r$ differ by a quantity bounded away from zero. Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-packet-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-packet-estimates">27</a> makes all these cross terms smaller than any fixed power of $r$. Terms containing only special packets are $O(r^2)$, and the remainder terms are $o(r)$. Consequently, at *every* intermediate probe prefix, $$\label{eq:seed-huge-imaginary}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v_{P,r}^{(j)},v_r\rangle=o(r).$$ More quantitatively, let $\varepsilon_r=o(r)$ bound the norm of the discarded vacuum remainder at the start of the probe sequence. For every fixed $M$, the preceding argument gives $$\label{eq:seed-huge-imaginary-modulus}
 \max_j\lvert \mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v_{P,r}^{(j)},v_r\rangle\rvert
 \leq C_M\bigl(r^2+\varepsilon_r+r^M\bigr).$$ The constant depends on the finite pulse list, a positive lower bound on center separations, and finitely many designated vacuum-tail seminorms. The exact probe prefixes preserve the norm of the discarded remainder; no number-operator estimate is imposed on it. This derivation used only center preservation, so there is no circular small-phase assumption.

<a id="eq:seed-probe-direction-change"></a>
By <a href="#eq:seed-packet-probe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-packet-probe">[eq:seed-packet-probe]</a>, an ordinary center $-tyv_r$ therefore has spin phase $o(r)$ in every probe. In either geometry a probe acts as identity plus $O(r)$ on the ordinary part and on that part with any spin matrix inserted. On the special part the norm is $O(r)$, so unitarity suffices. Using $$v_{P,r}^{(j+1)}-v_{P,r}^{(j)}
 =(V_ju_r)^*\bigl(V^*PV-P\bigr)(V_ju_r)\boldsymbol\Omega$$ for the next probe $V$, or equivalently estimating $(PV-VP)(V_ju_r)\boldsymbol\Omega$, gives $$\label{eq:seed-probe-direction-change}
 \lVert v_{P,r}^{(j)}-v_{P,r}^{(0)}\rVert=O(r)$$ for the fixed finite list.

After removing an ordinary center, expand the displacement in each probe to first order. Equation <a href="#eq:seed-huge-imaginary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-huge-imaginary">[eq:seed-huge-imaginary]</a> supplies only an $o(r)$ additional phase in the huge-cost geometry. Replacing $v_{P,r}^{(j)}$ by its starting value in the first-order displacement costs $O(r^2)$, by <a href="#eq:seed-probe-direction-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-probe-direction-change">[eq:seed-probe-direction-change]</a>. The first-order sum is therefore $$-\mathrm ir\sum_{a=1}^2
 \left(\sum_{k:a_k=a}\tau_k\right)P_aq(v_{P_a,r}^{(0)})=0.$$ Here is a uniform remainder bound for this cancellation. For a fixed ordinary center $d_r$, let $\mathcal B_r(d_r)$ be the net probe action conjugated by $W(d_r/r)$, and put $$\Delta_r=\max_j\lVert v_{P_j,r}^{(j)}-v_{P_j,r}^{(0)}\rVert,\qquad
 \alpha_r(d_r)=\max_j
       2\lvert \tau_j\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v_{P_j,r}^{(j)},d_r\rangle\rvert.$$ Then for every designated centered tail $\eta$, <a id="eq:seed-uniform-probe-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:seed-uniform-probe-bound}
 \lVert (\mathcal B_r(d_r)-\mathbf 1)\eta\rVert
 \leq C_K\left[
       (r^2+r\Delta_r)\lVert \eta\rVert_1
           +\alpha_r(d_r)\lVert \eta\rVert\right].$$ Indeed, remove each spin phase by its operator-norm bound and replace each displacement direction by its starting value. The latter replacement costs at most $C r\Delta_r\lVert \eta\rVert_1$ through the finite product. In the product with starting directions, hold those directions fixed at their given $r$-values and introduce an auxiliary pulse strength $\lambda$. Its first derivative at $\lambda=0$ is zero, by the clocks. Its second derivative is a finite sum controlled by $\lVert q(v)q(w)\eta\rVert\leq C\lVert v\rVert\lVert w\rVert\lVert \eta\rVert_1$; the intervening displacements preserve this seminorm uniformly. Evaluating the Taylor remainder at $\lambda=r$ gives $C_Kr^2\lVert \eta\rVert_1$; no differentiability of the directions in $r$ is assumed. The constant $C_K$ depends only on the fixed pulse list; all actual insertion directions have norm one. At a standard center $\alpha_r=0$. At a huge-cost center <a href="#eq:seed-huge-imaginary-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-huge-imaginary-modulus">[eq:seed-huge-imaginary-modulus]</a> gives $\alpha_r\leq C(r^2+\varepsilon_r+r^M)=o(r)$. Together with $\Delta_r=O(r)$, this proves the claimed uniform $o(r)$ estimate using the single tail seminorm $\lVert \eta\rVert_1$. It applies unchanged to a root-spin-inserted tail. If the input also contains a discarded norm remainder $\rho_r$, its contribution to the net identity error is at most $2\lVert \rho_r\rVert$, by unitarity. No field operator is applied to such a remainder in this argument.

On a special packet the change of its phase coefficient during the sequence is $O(r)$, by <a href="#eq:seed-probe-direction-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-probe-direction-change">[eq:seed-probe-direction-change]</a> and the boundedness of its scaled center. The small displacements contribute $O(r)$ on its tail, and its starting phase coefficients converge to the prescribed $c_{j,a}$. The resulting finite spin product thus converges to <a href="#eq:seed-zero-clocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-zero-clocks">[eq:seed-zero-clocks]</a>. Multiplication by the packet’s amplitude $O(r)$ makes its error $o(r)$. All quantities defining the probes came from the vacuum. The estimates after removing a center, however, use only the tail seminorms. They therefore apply to every bounded input family with the same center list. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### The packet invariant and the singular echo

We now apply the selective estimates to the exact initialized decomposition and propagate it through costs, mixers, and simulated seed gates. The required statements include moving input vectors: later insertion estimates must be allowed to use the approximating circuit itself as an input.

We call the times between simulated gates *standard stages*. A second kind of stage occurs immediately after the forward cost in a seed echo. The induction will maintain the following assertions, with the same center list for every bounded input family.

<a id="eq:seed-low-ordinary"></a>
1.  At a standard stage there is a unitary $A_r$, a finite product of root spin rotations and spin-controlled Weyl displacements with uniformly bounded directions, and a scalar $\zeta_r$ of modulus one, such that $$\label{eq:seed-low-ordinary}
     \Psi_{O,r}(\eta_r)=\zeta_r A_r\eta_r+o(r).$$ The operators $A_r,A_r^*$ preserve all top-number seminorms uniformly.

2.  The special part has a finite packet expansion up to $o(r)$, with amplitude $r$, bounded tails, scaled centers <a id="eq:seed-special-centers"></a>$$\label{eq:seed-special-centers}
     d_{zy,r}=-2bz e+yD_r,\qquad z,y\in\{-1,1\},$$ and output spin $y$. Coincident centers cause no problem. The vector $D_r$ is a finite sum of actual earlier insertion directions with fixed bounded coefficients. Each such direction is within $O(r)$ of a bounded family in $\mathcal S$.

<a id="eq:seed-transport"></a>
3.  At a standard stage the special insertion is transported: $$\label{eq:seed-transport}
     u_r^*Z\psi_{S,r}/r=w_r+o(1),$$ where $w_r$ is the initial vector <a href="#eq:seed-address-direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-address-direction">[eq:seed-address-direction]</a>. Between a special flip and its undoing the right-hand side instead has a minus sign.

Here and below the norm remainders need not have bounded number moments. They are kept as separate remainder vectors and carried through later unitaries by their norm bounds. Only the designated tails require moment estimates.

The initialization satisfies this invariant with $A_r=U_{O,r}$, $D_r=0$, and $\zeta_r=e^{\mathrm i\vartheta_r}$: its ordinary amplitude differs from one by $O(r^2)$. Equation <a href="#eq:seed-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-transport">[eq:seed-transport]</a> is initially exact. The following observation will be used repeatedly.

<a id="stmt-lem-seed-low-insertions"></a>
<div id="lem:seed-low-insertions" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Insertion approximation from forward action). *If <a href="#eq:seed-exact-special-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-exact-special-norm">[eq:seed-exact-special-norm]</a> and <a href="#eq:seed-low-ordinary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-low-ordinary">[eq:seed-low-ordinary]</a> hold uniformly on bounded input families, then <a id="eq:seed-insertion-O-r"></a>$$\label{eq:seed-insertion-O-r}
 \lVert u_r^*Pu_r\boldsymbol\Omega-A_r^*PA_r\boldsymbol\Omega\rVert=O(r)
 \qquad(P=Y,Z).$$ In particular the actual insertion directions have bounded smooth approximants. No approximation to $u_r^*$ on a high packet is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-low-insertions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-low-insertions</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\widehat v_{P,r}=A_r^*PA_r\boldsymbol\Omega$. This is a bounded smooth family. Unitarity and $A_r\widehat v_{P,r}=PA_r\boldsymbol\Omega$ give $$\lVert u_r^*Pu_r\boldsymbol\Omega-\widehat v_{P,r}\rVert
 =\lVert Pu_r\boldsymbol\Omega-u_r\widehat v_{P,r}\rVert
 \leq\lVert P(u_r-\zeta_rA_r)\boldsymbol\Omega\rVert
    +\lVert (u_r-\zeta_rA_r)\widehat v_{P,r}\rVert.$$ Each term is $O(r)$ by the exact special norm and the ordinary approximation. The possibly nonconvergent scalar phase cancels. ◻

</details>

For this induction, assume that the nonzero projection hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-selective-accuracy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-selective-accuracy">30</a> hold at every required probe start. We call these the *visibility conditions*. The final subsection proves that one perturbation of the finite target word ensures all of them and then constructs the probes in order.

An unscaled cost uses the actual insertion $v_{Z,r}$ and simply left-multiplies $A_r$ by $e^{-\mathrm icZq(v_{Z,r})}$. On a special packet with spin $y$, it adds a bounded displacement to the tail and a scalar phase, leaving its scaled center and spin unchanged. It commutes with $Z$, so it preserves the transport identity exactly.

To implement a bulk mixer $R_\theta=e^{-\mathrm i\theta X}$, first use selective probes to apply $R_\theta^{-1}$ on the special packets, then apply the global mixer $R_\theta$. On ordinary packets the first step is identity to $o(r)$, so the low update is $R_\theta$. On special packets the two spin actions cancel to $o(1)$. The same is true on their $Z$-inserted tails. Thus the special center and spin return to their initial values, and transport is preserved to $o(1)$ after division by the special amplitude $r$.

<a id="eq:seed-echo-list"></a>
For a longitudinal seed gate use a fixed $t\neq0$ and the four operations $$\label{eq:seed-echo-list}
 \text{cost }t/r,\qquad
 \text{selective }(-\mathrm iX),\qquad
 \text{cost }-t/r,\qquad
 \text{selective }(\mathrm iX).$$ The next lemma verifies both the singular cancellation and its error scale.

<a id="stmt-lem-seed-one-echo"></a>
<div id="lem:seed-one-echo" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:seed-echo-low"></a>
<a id="eq:seed-D-update"></a>
**Lemma 32** (One echo). *Under the invariant and the visibility conditions, the echo <a href="#eq:seed-echo-list" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-echo-list">[eq:seed-echo-list]</a> preserves the invariant. Up to a scalar of modulus one its low update is $$\label{eq:seed-echo-low}
 A_r\longmapsto W_Z(g_r)A_r,
 \qquad
 g_r=\frac{t}{r}(v'_r-v_r)=-2t w_r+o(1),$$ where $v_r$ is the actual $Z$-insertion before the forward cost and $v'_r$ is the actual insertion immediately after the selective flip. The special-center update can be taken to be exactly $$\label{eq:seed-D-update}
 D_r\longmapsto D_r-t(v_r+v'_r).$$ All ordinary errors are $o(r)$ before and after the large costs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-one-echo"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-one-echo</summary>

*Proof.* Write $u_{\mathrm{in},r}$ for the exact prefix before the echo, and $V_r$ for its selective flip. The next two prefixes are $$u_{\mathrm{cost},r}=e^{-\mathrm itZq(v_r)/r}u_{\mathrm{in},r},
 \qquad u_{\mathrm{flip},r}=V_r u_{\mathrm{cost},r}.$$ Their actual $Z$-insertions satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 u_{\mathrm{in},r}^*Zu_{\mathrm{in},r}\boldsymbol\Omega
 &=u_{\mathrm{cost},r}^*Zu_{\mathrm{cost},r}\boldsymbol\Omega=v_r,\\
 u_{\mathrm{flip},r}^*Zu_{\mathrm{flip},r}\boldsymbol\Omega&=v'_r.
 \end{aligned}$$ The first equality holds because the forward cost commutes with the root $Z$. It splits the ordinary part into spins $y$ with centers $-tyv_r$, and changes each special center to $d_{zy,r}-tyv_r$. Centered tails remain bounded. The standard-stage imaginary projection against $v_r$ is unchanged by adding $-tyv_r$. Thus the assumed special visibility separates special and ordinary centers at this huge-cost stage.

<a id="eq:seed-flip-change"></a>
Denote the exact ordinary and special components after that forward cost by $\Psi^{\mathrm{cost}}_{O,r}$ and $\psi^{\mathrm{cost}}_{S,r}$, respectively. Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-selective-accuracy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-selective-accuracy">30</a> gives net identity to $o(r)$ on the ordinary part, including its $Z$-inserted version. On the special part it gives $-\mathrm iX$ to error $o(r)$, again including the $Z$-inserted version. Since $XZX=-Z$, it follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 v'_r-v_r
 &=u_{\mathrm{cost},r}^*(V_r^*ZV_r-Z)
      (\Psi^{\mathrm{cost}}_{O,r}(\boldsymbol\Omega)+\psi^{\mathrm{cost}}_{S,r})\notag\\
 &=-2u_{\mathrm{cost},r}^*Z\psi^{\mathrm{cost}}_{S,r}+o(r)
 =-2r w_r+o(r).
\label{eq:seed-flip-change}
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality uses transport, which the forward cost preserves. The same calculation shows that transport changes sign at this flip.

<a id="eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation"></a>
On an ordinary input the reverse cost combines with the forward cost as $$\label{eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation}
 e^{\mathrm itZq(v'_r)/r}e^{-\mathrm itZq(v_r)/r}
 =\xi_r W_Z\!\left(\frac{t}{r}(v'_r-v_r)\right),
 \qquad |\xi_r|=1.$$ The Weyl phase $\xi_r$ is independent of the root spin because the two spin factors multiply to $Z^2=\mathbf 1$. It may fail to converge. Equation <a href="#eq:seed-flip-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-flip-change">[eq:seed-flip-change]</a> makes the residual direction bounded and proves <a href="#eq:seed-echo-low" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-echo-low">[eq:seed-echo-low]</a>. The ordinary $o(r)$ probe error is carried through the reverse cost by unitarity. No insertion approximation has been divided by $r$: the cancellation in <a href="#eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation">[eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation]</a> used the two actual directions, and only then was <a href="#eq:seed-flip-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-flip-change">[eq:seed-flip-change]</a> invoked.

A special packet has spin $-y$ between the flips. Its two cost displacements therefore add as $-tyv_r-tyv'_r$, giving <a href="#eq:seed-D-update" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-D-update">[eq:seed-D-update]</a>. Here $y$ remains its label from the standard stage before the echo; it is not relabeled when the spin flips. Its scalar phases are harmless. The second selective flip is applied at a standard ordinary geometry, since <a href="#eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation">[eq:seed-exact-echo-cancellation]</a> has already removed the huge ordinary centers. It acts trivially to $o(r)$ on ordinary tails, restores special spin $y$, and restores the plus sign in transport. At this second-flip probe start the exact special norm is still $r$, and the ordinary low action already has the updated unitary $W_Z(g_r)A_r$. Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-low-insertions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-low-insertions">31</a> consequently approximates the actual insertion to $O(r)$ by this updated low insertion, irrespective of the temporarily negative transport sign.

The special insertion remains transported because each cost commutes with $Z$, while each selective flip anticommutes with $Z$ on the special component to error $o(r)$. In particular, this proof needs only forward estimates on that component. Boundedness of the residual direction implies uniform moment bounds for the new $A_r,A_r^*$. Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-low-insertions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-low-insertions">31</a> supplies bounded approximants to the next actual insertion. Finally, $v'_r-v_r=O(r)$, so the updated $D_r$ retains the asserted bounded-approximant property. ◻

</details>

This induction also proves its claimed uniformity on input families. The exact decomposition <a href="#eq:seed-exact-special-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-exact-special-norm">[eq:seed-exact-special-norm]</a> is linear in the input. At initialization every input has the same four special centers. Unscaled costs and compensated mixers preserve that center list, and <a href="#eq:seed-D-update" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-D-update">[eq:seed-D-update]</a> updates it independently of the tail. The probe parameters are determined on the vacuum, but Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-selective-accuracy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-selective-accuracy">30</a> acts uniformly on the whole family of tails having these centers. Every discarded vector has norm $o(r)$ and is propagated by exact unitaries. Since the total gate and probe lists are fixed and finite before $r\downarrow0$, their errors add to $o(r)$. This establishes the invariant with no assertion about number moments of the discarded remainders.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### The external-mode limit and the energy pairing

The echo has now produced the bounded direction $-2t w_r+o(1)$. It remains to show that this direction acts as an independent Gaussian seed, and that the resulting identification preserves root-shift energy. Ordinary Gram convergence alone does not give the latter conclusion, so we compute the root-shift pairing explicitly.

We now identify the limiting low circuit and at the same time establish the limits of the projections used to select the probes. Introduce at each vertex a two-dimensional real external mode space with orthonormal vectors $\ell,w$, and let $E_{\mathrm{ext}}=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}_{\mathbb C}\{\ell,w\}$. Its Gaussian position operators commute and are independent standard Gaussians in the vacuum. To specify the recursion by height, put $$\mathcal H_{\mathrm{seed},0}=\mathbb C^2\otimes\Gamma_s(E_{\mathrm{ext}}),
 \qquad
 \mathcal H_{\mathrm{seed},h+1}
 =\mathbb C^2\otimes\Gamma_s\!\left(
    \mathcal H_{\mathrm{seed},h}^{\circ}\oplus E_{\mathrm{ext}}\right).$$ The vacuum is $\lvert +\rangle\otimes\Omega$, and empty descendants give the same compatible height embeddings as in the foundations. In the mode space at height $h+1$, the first summand contains the whole centered child rooted space, including that child’s seeds. The second summand contains the two new seeds at the current root. Equivalently, the root Hilbert space is the spin times the pointed Gaussian space of these two seeds times the symmetric Fock space over centered children.

<a id="eq:seed-formal-initialization"></a>
The formal low circuit starts with $$\label{eq:seed-formal-initialization}
 A^{\mathrm{form}}=R_\star e^{2\mathrm ibZq(\ell)}.$$ A bulk mixer and an unscaled cost have their usual seeded rules. An echo with parameter $t$ has the formal update $e^{-2\mathrm it Zq(w)}$. At each vertex, $q(w)$ is the Gaussian seed of the requested word: the same coordinate is reused at every seed gate, while different vertices have independent copies. The coordinate $q(\ell)$ is independent of those seeds and enters through initialization; its effect is removed by taking $b$ small in the final proof. Thus an echo with $t=\sigma/2$ has the seed law of the requested gate of angle $\sigma$, not a newly sampled seed at each pulse. The root-shift calculation below identifies the energy, which is not determined by this marginal law alone.

At a standard stage write $v_P^{\mathrm{form}}=(A^{\mathrm{form}})^*P
A^{\mathrm{form}}\boldsymbol\Omega$, with the appropriate accumulated word in $A^{\mathrm{form}}$. Treat these insertions and $e=Z\boldsymbol\Omega$ as endogenous child directions. In particular their inner products with the external directions $\ell,w$ are zero.

<a id="stmt-lem-seed-gram-induction"></a>
<div id="lem:seed-gram-induction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 33** (Finite Gram induction). *Suppose the required selective probe lists have been constructed through a given standard stage. The joint Gram matrix of $e$, all actual standard-stage insertion vectors used so far, and $\ell_r,w_r$, converges to that of the corresponding formal endogenous directions and the two orthogonal external directions. The limiting energy of the actual insertion converges to the energy of the formal seeded insertion. The same assertions hold at the standard ordinary geometry just before the second flip in an echo, with the temporary transport sign retained.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-gram-induction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-gram-induction</summary>

*Proof.* Every $A_r$ in the invariant is a finite sum of root spin matrices times Weyl displacements. Its directions are finite linear combinations of earlier actual insertion directions, $d_{0,r}$, and the bounded echo directions $g_r$. Its adjoint and its insertion vectors have the same finite-Weyl-sum form. The number of summands is finite for the fixed circuit; no bound uniform in circuit length is required.

<a id="eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram"></a>
For such sums all vacuum inner products reduce to $$\label{eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram}
 \langle W(f)\Omega,W(g)\Omega\rangle
 =\exp\!\left(-\tfrac12\lVert g-f\rVert^2
                 +\mathrm i\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle f,g\rangle\right).$$ Thus only finitely many direction inner products are needed at a step. The initial directions satisfy $d_{0,r}=2b\ell_r+o(1)$, and the echo directions satisfy $g_r=-2tw_r+o(1)$. Norm-small errors in these bounded directions may be removed using Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-packet-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-packet-estimates">27</a>. Every actual standard insertion differs by $O(r)$ from its bounded smooth low insertion, by Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-low-insertions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-low-insertions">31</a>. Thus, when computing a new Gram entry, an earlier actual insertion may first be replaced by its low insertion at error $O(r)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-high-directions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-high-directions">28</a> therefore makes its pairing with $\ell_r,w_r$ vanish. Together with $\langle \ell_r,w_r\rangle\to0$, this is exactly the orthogonal-sum rule for external and endogenous directions. Formula <a href="#eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram">[eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram]</a> now proves the Gram assertion inductively.

Here is the additional calculation for the root-shift energy, so that ordinary Gram convergence is not silently substituted for convergence of a different operator. A low $Y$- or $Z$-insertion is centered: the circuit preserves the joint spin-flip parity and these matrices are odd. Write such an insertion as $$h=\sum_{j=1}^J s_j\otimes W(f_j)\Omega,
 \qquad s_j\in\mathbb C^2.$$ In the unseeded representation, $\mathsf Lh=\lvert +\rangle\otimes a^*(h)\Omega$. The one-particle coefficient of $W(f)\Omega$ is $\mathrm ie^{-\lVert f\rVert^2/2}f$. Consequently <a id="eq:seed-energy-weyl-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:seed-energy-weyl-sum}
 \langle h,\mathsf Lh\rangle
 =-\mathrm i\sum_{j=1}^J
       \langle s_j,+\rangle\,e^{-\lVert f_j\rVert^2/2}\langle f_j,h\rangle.$$ Each pairing $\langle f_j,h\rangle$ is one of the same finite linear combinations of insertion and external-direction Grams. In the formal seeded representation the same formula holds, with $h$ inserted in the endogenous summand; the external components of $f_j$ then pair to zero. More explicitly, the external two-mode Fock factor represents the Gaussian variables at the root, while the child Fock factor is built over endogenous directions. The root shift puts the root-seed factor in its pointed vacuum and creates $h$ only in that child factor. The two-factor decomposition is identified with Fock space over their orthogonal direct sum. The high-direction argument gives exactly this zero pairing in the actual limit. Finally, the actual insertion differs in norm from $h$ by $O(r)$. Since $\mathsf L$ is an isometry, $$\lvert \mathcal E(v)-\mathcal E(h)\rvert
 \leq(\lVert v\rVert+\lVert h\rVert)\lVert v-h\rVert,$$ which proves energy convergence. A finite scalar bulk phase cancels from every insertion. The same argument applies just before an echo’s second flip, because its ordinary centers have already canceled and its low update has already been made. ◻

</details>

The standard-stage Gram limits determine the projections needed for ordinary probe starts. For a probe immediately after a cost of size $t/r$, we also need the following transverse projection estimate.

<a id="stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse"></a>
<div id="lem:seed-huge-transverse" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (The huge-stage transverse projection). *Immediately after a forward cost with fixed $t\neq0$, the actual $Y$-insertion has vanishing pairing with every bounded family in $\mathcal S$. In particular, its pairing with every special scaled center tends to zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse</summary>

*Proof.* For a bounded input $\eta_r$, use the standard-stage uniform forward approximation on both $\boldsymbol\Omega$ and $\eta_r$. After the huge cost their ordinary pieces have centers $-tyv_r$ and root spins $y$. In the pairing defining $\langle u_r^*Yu_r\boldsymbol\Omega,\eta_r\rangle$, the inserted $Y$ reverses the spin. Nonzero spin pairings therefore require the two opposite ordinary centers. Their distance is $2|t|$, so their contribution vanishes by <a href="#eq:seed-packet-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-packet-separation">[eq:seed-packet-separation]</a>. Every contribution with a special component is $O(r)$; the ordinary remainders are $o(r)$. This proves the first assertion. Before the huge cost, the invariant approximates every special center by a bounded smooth vector to $O(r)$. In particular the intermediate directions $v'_r$ in <a href="#eq:seed-D-update" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-D-update">[eq:seed-D-update]</a> differ by $O(r)$ from the corresponding earlier standard insertions, by <a href="#eq:seed-flip-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-flip-change">[eq:seed-flip-change]</a>. Adding the forward displacement $-tyv_r$ preserves this property. The first assertion and Cauchy–Schwarz prove the second. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Visibility and the order of construction

<a id="eq:seed-formal-visibility"></a>
The preceding arguments are conditional only on the visibility conditions. We now choose the target angles and all finite probe lists so that those conditions hold. The order is important: first use the formal low circuit to perturb the target angles, then construct the probe lists successively from limits established at earlier stages, and only afterwards choose $r$. Fix a finite seeded word, regard all its angles as independent real variables $\lambda$, and replace each seed gate of angle $\sigma$ formally by an echo parameter $t=\sigma/2$. Include in a finite list all standard-stage probe starts: compensated mixers and the second flips of echoes. Also include the standard stage preceding each forward huge cost. At a second-flip probe start the current echo’s reverse cost has already occurred: count that echo in the center accumulator and include its seed displacement in the formal ordinary state. Its packet label $y$ is still the label before the echo, although its current spin is $-y$. Define, at each probe start, $$D^{\mathrm{form}}
 =-2\sum_{j:\,\text{reverse cost already applied}}
       t_jv_{Z,j}^{\mathrm{form}},$$ where the insertion is taken immediately before that echo. At each listed stage and for each $z,y$, impose $$\label{eq:seed-formal-visibility}
 a_{zy}^{\mathrm{form}}(\lambda)
 =-2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle v_Z^{\mathrm{form}},-2bz e+yD^{\mathrm{form}}\rangle\neq0.$$

Each function in <a href="#eq:seed-formal-visibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-formal-visibility">[eq:seed-formal-visibility]</a> is real analytic in the finitely many angles. One may verify this without an unbounded-operator analyticity theorem: expand the finite word into Weyl sums and use <a href="#eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram">[eq:seed-vacuum-weyl-gram]</a> recursively. These expressions use finitely many sums, products, and exponentials of previous finite Gram expressions.

None of these analytic functions is identically zero. To see this, set *all* angles of the target word to zero, while keeping the initialization fixed. Every listed formal standard stage is then <a href="#eq:seed-formal-initialization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-formal-initialization">[eq:seed-formal-initialization]</a>, and $D^{\mathrm{form}}=0$. A direct one-Gaussian calculation gives $$\langle v_Z^{\mathrm{form}},e\rangle
 =\mathrm ie^{-8b^2},\qquad
 a_{zy}^{\mathrm{form}}=4bz e^{-8b^2}\neq0.$$ The temporary minus sign in special transport before a second flip does not change this formal ordinary insertion or its center formula. Thus the same test point works for every listed stage.

The complement of the finite union of these analytic zero sets, and of the hyperplanes $t_j=0$, is dense. Perturb the target angles arbitrarily little into that complement. Energy changes arbitrarily little because it is continuous by the same finite-Weyl-sum formulas.

We can now construct the actual pulses successively. At a standard probe start, Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-gram-induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-gram-induction">33</a> supplies the limits of both special projection coordinates, and <a href="#eq:seed-formal-visibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-formal-visibility">[eq:seed-formal-visibility]</a> makes their first coordinate nonzero. Choose the finite ensemble-control list from these limiting pairs. Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-selective-accuracy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-selective-accuracy">30</a> implements it with the required errors. At a huge-stage first flip, the first coordinate is unchanged by the forward displacement, while the second coordinate tends to zero by Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-huge-transverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-huge-transverse">34</a>. Its limiting pairs are therefore $(a_{zy}^{\mathrm{form}},0)$, again nonzero. They too admit the ensemble-control construction. Continue the packet and Gram inductions to the next stage. The argument at the second flip uses the already updated formal low circuit, with the sign of transport still negative until that flip is undone. In particular no pulse list is chosen using a limit that presupposes its own implementation.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:seed-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:seed-simulation">26</a>.* Start with the requested finite seeded word and a tolerance. For a small fixed $b>0$, prepend $R_\star e^{2\mathrm ib Zq(\ell)}$ in the formal seeded calculus, with $\ell$ an additional independent Gaussian coordinate at every site. As $b\downarrow0$, its energy tends to that of the original seeded word. Indeed, finite bounded-direction Weyl words are strongly continuous in this initial displacement, as are their insertion and energy Grams. At $b=0$ the initial global mixer acts on the original $\lvert +\rangle$ product state by a scalar, so it changes no expectation; the extra Gaussian coordinate is then unused.

Choose $b$ sufficiently small for this error, and perturb the target angles sufficiently little that all the visibility conditions hold, with another arbitrarily small energy error. Fix the resulting finite probe lists by the successive construction above. With these lists fixed, take $r>0$ sufficiently small. The packet induction and Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-gram-induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-gram-induction">33</a> show that the ordinary word consisting of the initialization, unscaled costs, compensated mixers, and seed echoes has energy arbitrarily close to that of the perturbed formal seeded word. Summing the three errors proves the assertion.

Every gate used is a cost or mixer pulse, possibly with negative angle. Toggled probes and all compensations are finite such words; consecutive gates of the same kind can be combined, or zero-angle gates inserted to obtain the stipulated alternating QAOA format. After $b$, the perturbation, the finite probe lists, and $r$ have been chosen, the entire word and its angles are fixed. Proposition <a href="#prop:tree-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tree-transfer">3</a> therefore applies before any approximation parameter is changed. This proves the claimed order of limits and parameter independence. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### The ordinary word and the SK limit

The preceding constructions are now fixed in their order of use. The following error allocation leaves every circuit finite before applying the tree-to-SK identity.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-huge-transverse</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:classical-near-optimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:classical-near-optimum">21</a> with error $\varepsilon/4$ and helper bound $\eta_H=(\varepsilon/8)^2$. It gives a finite bounded smooth classical calculation with output $m$ and helper probability $p_H$ satisfying $$\mathcal E(m)\ge P_*-\varepsilon/4,
 \qquad 2\sqrt{p_H}<\varepsilon/4.$$ Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:classical-synthesis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classical-synthesis">22</a> with $\eta=\varepsilon/8$. The resulting finite seeded circuit has insertion $v$ with $$\mathcal E(v)\ge\mathcal E(m)-2\eta-2\sqrt{p_H}
             >P_*-3\varepsilon/4.$$ By Theorem <a href="#thm:seed-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:seed-simulation">26</a>, a finite ordinary word approximates this seeded value to error less than $\varepsilon/4$. Its value is therefore greater than $P_*-\varepsilon$.

All choices just made concern limiting tree calculations and deterministic approximation parameters. After these choices there is a single finite word with real angles independent of $D$, $n$, and $J$. By Proposition <a href="#prop:tree-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tree-transfer">3</a>, its tree value equals its fixed-parameter SK value. Write the word in the form <a href="#eq:qaoa-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:qaoa-state">[eq:qaoa-state]</a>; it gives $v_p(\gamma,\beta)\ge P_*-\varepsilon$ for some finite $p$. The upper bound and monotonicity established above finish the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-order-of-choices"></a>
<div id="rem:order-of-choices" class="remark">

*Remark 35* (Order of choices). For the primary route the logical order is: accuracy; a fixed zero-temperature minimizer and stopping time $T<1$; a sufficiently fine finite scalar mesh at that $T$; finite sign smoothing; bounded column and trigonometric approximation; type exclusions; helper contractions and finite commutator approximations; elementary seed compilation; small initialization parameter $b$; generic angle perturbation; finite selective-probe lists; sufficiently small positive $r$; one fixed ordinary word; thermodynamic limit. Within any nested construction an outer accuracy is fixed before a finite approximation of its inner operations is chosen. No uniform depth estimate and no exchange of these limits is used. For the independent positive-temperature route, the initial scalar choices are instead a sufficiently large fixed finite $\beta_0$, a cutoff $T<q_{\beta_0}$, and a sufficiently fine finite message-passing discretization. Its degree limit is taken with those parameters fixed. The subsequent approximation and circuit choices are the same.

</div>

<a id="sec:regular-maxcut-proof"></a>
### Proof of the regular MaxCut consequence

We now prove Corollary <a href="#cor:regular-maxcut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:regular-maxcut">2</a>. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> provides one fixed word with SK value close to $P_*$, and Proposition <a href="#prop:tree-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tree-transfer">3</a> identifies that value with the large-degree positive-cost tree correlation. The proof first transfers this correlation to every graph satisfying the stated girth condition. It then uses local tree structure and the random-regular optimum asymptotics to obtain the separate successive-limit comparison.

<details id="proof-stmt-rem-order-of-choices"><summary>Proof of stmt-rem-order-of-choices</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:regular-maxcut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:regular-maxcut">2</a>.* The normalized negative cost used by Basso et al. <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BFMVZ2022](#ref-BFMVZ2022)] is $$C_D^-=-\frac1{\sqrt D}\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}Z_uZ_v
      =\frac2{\sqrt D}H_{\mathrm{MC}}(G)
       -\frac{|E|}{\sqrt D}I.$$ Thus its cost gate with angle $\gamma_j$ is, up to a scalar phase, the usual MaxCut cost gate with angle $2\gamma_j/\sqrt D$, with the same sign and unchanged mixer angle. Let $r_p^+(D,\gamma,\beta)$ be the distinguished-edge correlation for the positive tree cost in Proposition <a href="#prop:tree-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tree-transfer">3</a>, and put $$\nu_p(D,\gamma,\beta)=\frac{\sqrt D}{2}r_p^+(D,\gamma,\beta).$$ That proposition gives $\nu_p(D,\gamma,\beta)\to v_p(\gamma,\beta)$ for each fixed word.

For the observable at an edge, backward cancellation of commuting cost gates leaves only cost edges having an endpoint at distance at most $p-1$ from the distinguished edge’s endpoint set. An extra edge in this relevant cone would close a cycle of length at most $2p+1$. Under the stated girth condition the cone is therefore the regular tree cone. Conjugating *only this cone* by $X$ on one bipartition class reverses the cost sign and the distinguished $Z_uZ_v$, while preserving the mixers and the initial plus state. No bipartition of $G$ is used. Every edge therefore has negative-cost correlation $-r_p^+(D,\gamma,\beta)$, and <a id="eq:regular-tree-cut"></a>$$\label{eq:regular-tree-cut}
 q_{p,G}(2\gamma/\sqrt D,\beta)
 =\frac12+\frac{\nu_p(D,\gamma,\beta)}{\sqrt D}.$$ This is the finite-degree edge formula in the full version of <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[BFMVZ2022](#ref-BFMVZ2022), Equations (2.3)–(2.6) and (3.1)], in the convention of Proposition <a href="#prop:tree-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tree-transfer">3</a>. Choose the word by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> with $v_p(\gamma,\beta)\ge P_*-\eta/4$. For all sufficiently large $D$, fixed-word convergence gives $\nu_p(D,\gamma,\beta)\ge P_*-\eta/2$, proving <a href="#eq:high-girth-maxcut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:high-girth-maxcut">[eq:high-girth-maxcut]</a>.

For fixed $D,p$, let $b_{p,D}(G)$ be the fraction of edges whose relevant cone is not a regular tree cone. A conservative radius-$(p+1)$ neighborhood test bounds the number of such edges by a constant depending on $D,p$ times the number of cycles of length at most $2p+3$. Those fixed-length cycle counts are tight in the uniform simple regular model by McKay, Wormald, and Wysocka <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[MWW2004](#ref-MWW2004), Theorem 1]. Consequently $b_{p,D}(G_{n,D+1})\to0$ in graph probability and in mean as $n\to\infty$. Since each edge contributes a cut probability in $[0,1]$, the exact value on the other edges gives $$\left|q_{p,G}(2\gamma/\sqrt D,\beta)
 -\frac12-\frac{\nu_p(D,\gamma,\beta)}{\sqrt D}\right|
 \le b_{p,D}(G).$$ This proves convergence of the conditional quantum expectation to the tree value at fixed $D,p$; the whole random graph need not have large girth.

Dembo, Montanari, and Sen <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[DMS2017](#ref-DMS2017), Theorem 1.6(b), with the convention after Equation (1.7)] identify the optimal leading correction for the same simple regular model. After dividing their cut size by $nd/2$ and putting $d=D+1$, their theorem says that $\sqrt D(\operatorname{OPT}(G_{n,D+1})-1/2)$ converges to $P_*$ in the same successive graph-probability sense; here $\sqrt{D/(D+1)}\to1$. Their $P_*$ is ours: pairing their ordered off-diagonal Gaussians and using sign symmetry gives <a href="#eq:sk-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sk-model">[eq:sk-model]</a> in law, while the diagonal is a mean-zero configuration-independent shift. For large $D$ the deterministic quantity $P_*-\nu_p(D,\gamma,\beta)$ is at most $\eta/2$. If $\sqrt D\,\Delta_D>\eta$, the absolute centered-optimum error or the absolute conditional-expectation error multiplied by $\sqrt D$ must therefore exceed $\eta/4$. The first probability vanishes in the stated successive limit by the cited optimum theorem, and the second vanishes in the inner $n$ limit by the preceding bound. This proves <a href="#eq:random-regular-maxcut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:random-regular-maxcut">[eq:random-regular-maxcut]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:positive-temperature-alternative"></a>
### The independent positive-temperature construction

This appendix constructs near-optimal bounded classical formulas without using the zero-temperature scalar companion. Its input is the positive-temperature Parisi measure at a fixed finite inverse temperature. The Parisi diffusion first gives an almost optimal martingale output. A finite-step tree calculation and a strong symmetric-power limit then place that output in the rooted Hilbert space. Finally, the bounded approximation and color-exclusion lemmas of Section <a href="#sec:classical-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-optimization">3</a> put it in the form required by Theorem <a href="#thm:classical-synthesis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:classical-synthesis">22</a>.

Besides providing an independent route to the same synthesis input, the appendix retains the finite-degree message calculation and the quantitative fixed-temperature mesh bounds. Temperature is chosen first and held fixed in those estimates. No passage from the log-cosh terminal datum here to the terminal datum $|x|$ is used.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### The positive-temperature variational input

The use of a Parisi diffusion and its gradient martingale to choose incremental message-passing coefficients follows the classical construction of Montanari <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Montanari2019](#ref-Montanari2019), Section 3.1, Lemmas 3.2–3.3], with the more general framework of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[AMS2020](#ref-AMS2020), Assumption 1 and Theorem 2]. We use the finite-tree calculation of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023)] and convert its output to the synthesis formulas below.

<a id="eq:positive-parisi-pde"></a>
<a id="eq:positive-parisi-value"></a>
We record explicitly the spin-glass results that enter the argument. For an inverse temperature $\beta_0>0$, let $\mu_{\beta_0}$ be the finite-temperature Parisi measure and put $$\gamma(t)=\beta_0\mu_{\beta_0}([0,t]).$$ In field coordinates appropriate to the normalization of this paper, the Parisi PDE and free energy are $$\begin{aligned}
 \phi_t(t,x)
   &=-\frac12\bigl(\phi_{xx}(t,x)+\gamma(t)\phi_x(t,x)^2\bigr),\\
 \phi(1,x)&=\frac1{\beta_0}\log\bigl(2\cosh(\beta_0x)\bigr).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:positive-parisi-pde}$$ $$F_{\beta_0}
   =\phi(0,0)-\frac12\int_0^1t\gamma(t)\,\mathrm dt
    =\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1{n\beta_0}
        \mathbb E\log\sum_{\sigma}\exp\bigl(\beta_0h_{n,J}(\sigma)\bigr).
 \label{eq:positive-parisi-value}$$ Equation <a href="#eq:positive-parisi-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-parisi-value">[eq:positive-parisi-value]</a> is the positive-temperature Parisi formula <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Talagrand2006](#ref-Talagrand2006), Theorem 1.1], written in the field coordinates of <a href="#eq:positive-parisi-pde" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-parisi-pde">[eq:positive-parisi-pde]</a>. The covariance of $h_{n,J}$ divided by $n$ is $(R_{12}^2-1/n)/2$, where $R_{12}=n^{-1}\sum_i\sigma_i^1\sigma_i^2$. The $O(1/n)$ term is allowed by that theorem, so its limiting covariance is $t^2/2$. The partition function here is the unnormalized sum over configurations.

This route does not need the zero-temperature variational formula, even for existence of the limit defining $P_*$. Put $A_n=n^{-1}\mathbb E\max_\sigma h_{n,J}(\sigma)$ and let $F_{n,\beta_0}$ denote the finite-$n$ expression on the right of <a href="#eq:positive-parisi-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-parisi-value">[eq:positive-parisi-value]</a>. The elementary log-sum bounds give $$F_{n,\beta_0}-\frac{\log2}{\beta_0}
 \le A_n\le F_{n,\beta_0}.$$ For every fixed finite $\beta_0$, the Parisi formula gives convergence of $F_{n,\beta_0}$. Hence $\limsup_n A_n-\liminf_n A_n\le(\log2)/\beta_0$. Letting $\beta_0$ tend to infinity proves convergence of $A_n$ and yields <a id="eq:positive-free-energy-bounds"></a>$$P_*\le F_{\beta_0}\le P_*+\frac{\log2}{\beta_0}.
 \label{eq:positive-free-energy-bounds}$$ A direct zero-temperature variational formula is also known <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[AC2017](#ref-AC2017), Theorem 1]; the argument here needs only the positive-temperature formula and the preceding squeeze. We use the following precise finite-temperature facts. In the conventions of <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Lopatto2026](#ref-Lopatto2026)], they are Theorem 1.1, Lemma 2.2, Proposition 2.4, and Lemma 2.5, respectively:

1.  If $\beta_0>1$, there is $q=q_{\beta_0}\in(0,1)$ such that $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_{\beta_0}=[0,q]$. The restriction below $q$ has a density in $C^\infty([0,q))$; the point $q$ is an atom.

<a id="eq:positive-parisi-diffusion"></a>
2.  If $X$ solves the stochastic differential equation driven by a standard Brownian motion $W$, $$\mathrm dX_t=\gamma(t)\phi_x(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt+\mathrm dW_t,
      \qquad X_0=0,
      \label{eq:positive-parisi-diffusion}$$ then $\mathbb E\phi_x(t,X_t)^2=t$ for every $t\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_{\beta_0}$.

3.  All positive-order spatial derivatives of $\phi$ are bounded and jointly continuous on $[0,1]\times\mathbb R$. The function $\phi$ is even in $x$, $|\phi_x|\le1$, and $\phi_{xx}>0$. These regularity statements also follow from <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[AC2015](#ref-AC2015), Propositions 1–2 and Appendix Proposition 5, (66)] after the same change of variables.

4.  Differentiating the PDE along <a href="#eq:positive-parisi-diffusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-parisi-diffusion">[eq:positive-parisi-diffusion]</a> gives $\mathrm d\phi_x(t,X_t)=\phi_{xx}(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dW_t$.

The self-consistency identity $\mathbb E\phi_x(t,X_t)^2=t$ at points $t\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_{\beta_0}$, including $t=0$, also follows from the variational argument of Jagannath and Tobasco <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[JT2017](#ref-JT2017), Proposition 1.1 and Corollary 3.10]. To check the conventions, if $u$ denotes the PDE solution with terminal condition $\log\cosh x$ and diffusion coefficient $\beta_0^2$ in <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Lopatto2026](#ref-Lopatto2026)], then $\phi(t,x)=\beta_0^{-1}\bigl(u(t,\beta_0x)+\log2\bigr)$. In particular, the full-support input used here concerns positive temperature; no support property of a limiting zero-temperature minimizer is assumed. The version of <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Lopatto2026](#ref-Lopatto2026)] used here is version 3: its Hamiltonian is written with independent Gaussian terms over all ordered pairs. Combining the two terms for each unordered pair gives the normalization <a href="#eq:sk-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sk-model">[eq:sk-model]</a>; the diagonal terms form a centered, configuration-independent Gaussian. They have zero contribution to expected pressure and do not change the Parisi minimizer.

Write <a id="eq:classical-coefficients"></a>$$m(t,x)=\phi_x(t,x),\qquad
 a(t,x)=\phi_{xx}(t,x),\qquad
 b(t,x)=\gamma(t)m(t,x).
 \label{eq:classical-coefficients}$$ On every $[0,T]$ with $T<q$, these functions have bounded spatial derivatives of every order. They are also Lipschitz in time there: the density statement makes $\gamma$ smooth on $[0,T]$, and the PDE expresses the time derivatives of $m$ and $a$ in terms of bounded spatial derivatives. In particular, $a$ and $b$ are bounded and globally Lipschitz in $x$, uniformly on $[0,T]$. The function $a$ is even in $x$ and $b$ is odd.

<a id="stmt-lem-positive-temperature-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:positive-temperature-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:positive-temperature-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:parisi-normalization-and-endpoint"></a>
**Lemma 36** (A positive-temperature value bound). *For every $\beta_0>1$ and $0<T<q$, $$\int_0^T\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt
 \ge P_* -\frac{1/4+\log2}{\beta_0}-(q-T).
 \label{eq:positive-temperature-bound}$$ Moreover, $$\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)^2=1\quad(0\le t\le q),
 \qquad 1-q\le\beta_0^{-1}.
 \label{eq:parisi-normalization-and-endpoint}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-positive-temperature-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-positive-temperature-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Symmetry gives $m(0,0)=0$. The martingale identity and Itô isometry therefore give $$\mathbb Em(t,X_t)^2=\int_0^t\mathbb Ea(s,X_s)^2\,\mathrm ds.$$ The left-hand side equals $t$ on $[0,q]$. Continuity and boundedness of $a$, together with continuity of $X$, prove the first assertion of <a href="#eq:parisi-normalization-and-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parisi-normalization-and-endpoint">[eq:parisi-normalization-and-endpoint]</a>, including both endpoints.

<a id="eq:parisi-endpoint-formula"></a>
Since $\gamma=\beta_0$ on $[q,1]$, the Cole–Hopf formula gives $$\phi(q,x)=\frac1{\beta_0}\log\bigl(2\cosh(\beta_0x)\bigr)
                  +\frac{\beta_0(1-q)}2,
 \quad
 m(q,x)=\tanh(\beta_0x),
 \quad
 a(q,x)=\beta_0\operatorname{sech}^2(\beta_0x).
 \label{eq:parisi-endpoint-formula}$$ Thus, by Cauchy–Schwarz and the identity just proved, $$1-q=\mathbb E\operatorname{sech}^2(\beta_0X_q)
 \le\bigl(\mathbb E\operatorname{sech}^4(\beta_0X_q)\bigr)^{1/2}
 =\beta_0^{-1}.$$

Itô’s formula applied to $X_tm(t,X_t)$ and $\phi(t,X_t)$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_0^q\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt
   &=\mathbb EX_qm(q,X_q)-\int_0^qt\gamma(t)\,\mathrm dt,\\
 \mathbb E\phi(q,X_q)
   &=\phi(0,0)+\frac12\int_0^qt\gamma(t)\,\mathrm dt.
\end{aligned}$$ The stochastic integrals have mean zero because $a,m,b$ are bounded and $X$ has finite moments. The elementary inequality $$x\tanh(\beta_0x)
 \ge\frac1{\beta_0}\log\bigl(2\cosh(\beta_0x)\bigr)
              -\frac{\log2}{\beta_0}$$ follows, for example, by differentiating the difference for $x\ge0$ and using evenness. Substitution of <a href="#eq:parisi-endpoint-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parisi-endpoint-formula">[eq:parisi-endpoint-formula]</a> consequently gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_0^q\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt
 &\ge\phi(0,0)-\frac12\int_0^qt\gamma(t)\,\mathrm dt
                -\frac{\beta_0(1-q)}2-\frac{\log2}{\beta_0}\\
 &=F_{\beta_0}-\frac{\beta_0(1-q)^2}{4}
                       -\frac{\log2}{\beta_0}\\
 &\ge P_*-\frac{1/4+\log2}{\beta_0}.
\end{aligned}$$ Finally $0\le\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)\le(\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)^2)^{1/2}=1$ on $[0,q]$, so removing $[T,q]$ costs at most $q-T$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
#### The finite-iteration tree calculation

Here we state the finite-step input from El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke with the indices and normalizations needed below. It is the general calculation in <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023), Sections 2–4.1], not that paper’s zero-temperature near-optimality theorem.

<a id="eq:eams-output"></a>
Fix $\beta_0>1$ and $0<T<q$. Choose $\delta>0$ and an integer $L\ge1$ with $L\delta\le T$. Put $t_r=r\delta$. On the $(D+1)$-regular tree, initialize independent standard Gaussian labels $g_i$ and set $u^0_{i\to j}=u_i^0=g_i$, $A^{-1}_{i\to j}=1$. Each outgoing column multiplies a message by a coefficient from the preceding step. This lag will make its newest Gaussian layer conditionally centered, as verified below. For $0\le r<L$ use the recursions <a id="eq:eams-message-recursion"></a><a id="eq:eams-cavity-euler"></a><a id="eq:eams-node-euler"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 u^{r+1}_{i\to j}
   &=\frac1{\sqrt D}\sum_{v\in\partial i\setminus\{j\}}
                       A^{r-1}_{v\to i}u^r_{v\to i},
 &u^{r+1}_i
   &=\frac1{\sqrt{D+1}}\sum_{v\in\partial i}
                       A^{r-1}_{v\to i}u^r_{v\to i},
 \label{eq:eams-message-recursion}\\
 x^{r+1}_{i\to j}
   &=x^r_{i\to j}+\delta b(t_r,x^r_{i\to j})
                       +\sqrt\delta\,u^{r+1}_{i\to j},
 &x^0_{i\to j}&=\sqrt\delta\,g_i,
 \label{eq:eams-cavity-euler}\\
 x^{r+1}_i
   &=x^r_i+\delta b(t_r,x^r_i)+\sqrt\delta\,u^{r+1}_i,
 &x^0_i&=\sqrt\delta\,g_i.
 \label{eq:eams-node-euler}
\end{aligned}$$ The coefficients, for $0\le r\le L$, are <a id="eq:eams-finite-normalization"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 c_{D,r}&=\bigl(\mathbb Ea(t_r,x^r_{i\to j})^2\bigr)^{-1/2},
 &A^r_{i\to j}&=c_{D,r}a(t_r,x^r_{i\to j}),
 &B^r_i&=c_{D,r}a(t_r,x^r_i),
 \label{eq:eams-finite-normalization}\\
 z_i^L&=\sqrt\delta\sum_{r=1}^LB^{r-1}_iu_i^r.
 \label{eq:eams-output}
\end{aligned}$$ The same constant, computed from the directed-edge law, occurs in $A^r$ and $B^r$. Its definition is recursive: $x^r_{i\to j}$ has already been constructed when $c_{D,r}$ is chosen. Strict positivity of $a$ ensures every denominator is positive.

<a id="eq:eams-conditional-innovation"></a>
The one-step lag in $A^{r-1}u^r$ is important. Let $\mathcal G_{i\to j}^r$ be the sigma-field of Gaussian labels at distance at most $r$ from $i$ in the component left after deleting $\{i,j\}$, including the label at $i$. The variables $x^{r-1}_{i\to j}$ and $A^{r-1}_{i\to j}$ are $\mathcal G_{i\to j}^{r-1}$-measurable. The next message is linear in the Gaussian labels on the new outer layer. More precisely, <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023), Lemma 3.2] gives, for $r\ge1$, $$\begin{aligned}
 u^r_{i\to j}\mid\mathcal G_{i\to j}^{r-1}
       &\sim N\bigl(0,(\tau^r_{i\to j})^2\bigr),\\
 (\tau^{r+1}_{i\to j})^2
       &=\frac1D\sum_{v\in\partial i\setminus\{j\}}
       (A^{r-1}_{v\to i})^2(\tau^r_{v\to i})^2,
       \qquad \tau^0=1.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:eams-conditional-innovation}$$ The variance recursion also holds at $r=0$. Thus $A^{r-1}u^r$ has conditional mean zero given this earlier cavity history. Its variance may depend on that history; finite-degree independence of successive messages is not being asserted. For $r=0$ the sent column is simply $g_i$.

<a id="eq:eams-limit-output"></a>
Let $U_0,U_1,\ldots$ be independent standard Gaussians and define <a id="eq:eams-limit-euler"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 X^\delta_0&=\sqrt\delta\,U_0,
 &X^\delta_{r+1}
  &=X^\delta_r+\delta b(t_r,X^\delta_r)+\sqrt\delta\,U_{r+1},
 \label{eq:eams-limit-euler}\\
 c_{\delta,r}&=\bigl(\mathbb Ea(t_r,X^\delta_r)^2\bigr)^{-1/2},
 &Z^\delta_L
  &=\sqrt\delta\sum_{r=1}^L
        c_{\delta,r-1}a(t_{r-1},X^\delta_{r-1})U_r.
 \label{eq:eams-limit-output}
\end{aligned}$$ At fixed $\beta_0,T,\delta,L$, the update functions and constants $c_{\delta,r}$ are deterministic. The variables $X^\delta_r$ and $Z^\delta_L$ are random functions of the displayed Gaussian family.

<a id="stmt-lem-eams-finite-step"></a>
<div id="lem:eams-finite-step" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:eams-normalizer-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:eams-corrected-value"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Finite-step limit). *For the preceding construction, as $D\to\infty$ at fixed $\delta,L$, <a id="eq:eams-distribution-limit"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 c_{D,r}&\longrightarrow c_{\delta,r},
 \qquad 0\le r\le L,
 \label{eq:eams-normalizer-limit}\\
 (u^0_i,\ldots,u^L_i)&\Longrightarrow(U_0,\ldots,U_L),
 \qquad z_i^L\Longrightarrow Z^\delta_L,
 \label{eq:eams-distribution-limit}\\
 \lim_{D\to\infty}\frac{D+1}{2\sqrt D}\mathbb Ez_o^Lz_v^L
   &=\delta\sum_{r=1}^{L-1}
               c_{\delta,r}\mathbb Ea(t_r,X^\delta_r),
 \qquad o\sim v.
 \label{eq:eams-corrected-value}
\end{aligned}$$ The sum in <a href="#eq:eams-corrected-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-corrected-value">[eq:eams-corrected-value]</a> is empty when $L=1$. The distributional statements include convergence of every fixed moment. The analogous joint Gaussian limit holds for a directed edge. Moreover $\mathbb E(Z^\delta_L)^2=L\delta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-eams-finite-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-eams-finite-step</summary>

*Proof.* We use the bounded-coefficient CLT and edge calculation in <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023), Assumptions 2–3, Theorem 3.1, Proposition 2.1, and Lemmas 4.2–4.3]. The verification below has two parts: first the degree-dependent normalizers and moment bounds, then the edge value with its finite-step index. The functions $a(t_r,\cdot)$ are bounded and Lipschitz, the drift is Lipschitz, and only finitely many $r$ occur. Initially $c_{D,0}=c_{\delta,0}$, and $x^r$ uses normalizers only through index $r-2$, so the normalization induction is not circular. Inductively, the Gaussian limit of the preceding messages and bounded continuity of $a^2$ give $$\mathbb Ea(t_r,x^r_{i\to j})^2\longrightarrow
 \mathbb Ea(t_r,X^\delta_r)^2>0.$$ Thus $c_{D,r}$ converges, and the normalized coefficients and their Lipschitz constants are bounded uniformly for all sufficiently large $D$. This verifies the boundedness and normalization hypotheses used in the finite-step CLT, including when its coefficients depend on $D$. One may equivalently run its induction with this triangular sequence: convergent deterministic coefficients do not affect any induction step. All fixed moments are uniformly bounded. Indeed, an update sums the columns $A^{r-1}_{v\to i}u^r_{v\to i}$ from disjoint child cavity trees. They are independent across children and centered by <a href="#eq:eams-conditional-innovation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-conditional-innovation">[eq:eams-conditional-innovation]</a>; the bounded multiplier is measurable before the innovation it multiplies. In this application their marginal centering also follows from seed parity, since $a$ is even and $b$ is odd. The moment inequality for sums of independent centered variables gives, for each integer $k\ge2$, $$\mathbb E\left|D^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^DY_j\right|^k
 \le C_k\left((\mathbb EY_1^2)^{k/2}
                  +D^{1-k/2}\mathbb E|Y_1|^k\right).$$ Induction over the finite number of updates proves uniform bounds of every order. They imply uniform integrability and hence the asserted moment convergence. The $x$ and $z$ conclusions follow from their continuous, polynomial-growth expressions in the messages.

<a id="eq:eams-node-cavity-bound"></a>
For the energy, put $$R_{D,\ell}=\mathbb E\left[
 B_o^{\ell-1}B_v^{\ell-1}
 A_{o\to v}^{\ell-2}A_{v\to o}^{\ell-2}
 u_{o\to v}^{\ell-1}u_{v\to o}^{\ell-1}\right].$$ Proposition 2.1 of <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023)], with its degree $k=D+1$, is the exact identity <a id="eq:eams-exact-edge-expansion"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{D+1}{2\sqrt D}\mathbb Ez_o^Lz_v^L
 &=\delta\sum_{\ell=2}^L
   \mathbb E\left[A^{\ell-2}_{v\to o}(u^{\ell-1}_{v\to o})^2
                           B_o^{\ell-1}B_v^{\ell-2}\right]\\
 &\quad+\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt D}\sum_{\ell=1}^L R_{D,\ell}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:eams-exact-edge-expansion}$$ The second line is $O(D^{-1/2})$ by the fixed moment bounds. To compare node and cavity terms, split off the missing neighbor: $$u_i^r=\sqrt{\frac D{D+1}}\,u_{i\to j}^r
       +\frac{A_{j\to i}^{r-2}u_{j\to i}^{r-1}}{\sqrt{D+1}}
 \qquad (1\le r\le L).$$ Its squared difference from $u_{i\to j}^r$ is $O(D^{-1})$. The two Euler recursions and the spatial Lipschitz bounds therefore give, by induction over this fixed finite number of steps, $$\mathbb E|x_i^r-x_{i\to j}^r|^2+
 \mathbb E|B_i^r-A_{i\to j}^r|^2\le \frac C D.
 \label{eq:eams-node-cavity-bound}$$ These are the comparisons in Lemma 4.2 of <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023)]; in particular, the use of the spatial Lipschitz bound for $a$ is explicit.

Here is the reduction in Lemma 4.3 of that paper in our indices. Set $s=\ell-1$ and $H=A_{v\to o}^{s-1}B_v^{s-1}B_o^s$. This product depends only on the depth-$(s-1)$ cavity labels on the $v$ side and the depth-$s$ cavity labels on the $o$ side. Conditioning on these labels in <a href="#eq:eams-conditional-innovation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-conditional-innovation">[eq:eams-conditional-innovation]</a> therefore gives $$\mathbb E\bigl[H((u_{v\to o}^s)^2-1)\bigr]
 =\mathbb E\bigl[H((\tau_{v\to o}^s)^2-1)\bigr].$$ The variance estimate in <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[EAMS2023](#ref-EAMS2023), Lemmas 3.2–3.4] is uniform for the present normalizers. Indeed, if $K\ge1$ bounds the coefficients at the fixed horizon and $\Delta_r=\mathbb E[((\tau^r_{i\to j})^2-1)^2]$, its proof uses only the variance recursion, independence of child cavities, and the exact normalization $\mathbb E(A^r_{i\to j})^2=1$ to give $$\Delta_{r+1}\le2K^4\Delta_r+\frac{2(K^4+1)}D,
 \qquad \Delta_0=0.$$ Thus $\Delta_s=O(D^{-1})$, and the preceding conditional expectation is $O(D^{-1/2})$ by Cauchy–Schwarz. Replacing the two node coefficients by their cavity counterparts using <a href="#eq:eams-node-cavity-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-node-cavity-bound">[eq:eams-node-cavity-bound]</a> now yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\bigl[H(u_{v\to o}^s)^2\bigr]
 &=\mathbb E\bigl[A_{o\to v}^s(A_{v\to o}^{s-1})^2\bigr]
       +O(D^{-1/2})\\
 &=\mathbb EA_{o\to v}^s\,\mathbb E\bigl[(A_{v\to o}^{s-1})^2\bigr]
       +O(D^{-1/2})\\
 &=\mathbb EB_o^s+O(D^{-1/2}).
\end{aligned}$$ The middle equality uses the exact independence of the two cavity trees, and the last uses their coefficient normalization and one more node-to-cavity comparison. This proves the quantitative replacement without a convergence rate for $c_{D,r}$. All constants here may depend on $\delta,L,\beta_0,T$, which are fixed. Taking the degree limit and setting $r=\ell-1$ proves <a href="#eq:eams-corrected-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-corrected-value">[eq:eams-corrected-value]</a>.[^1] Finally, the coefficients multiplying $U_r$ in <a href="#eq:eams-limit-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-limit-output">[eq:eams-limit-output]</a> are measurable with respect to $U_0,\ldots,U_{r-1}$. Orthogonality of martingale increments and the definition of $c_{\delta,r-1}$ give $\mathbb E(Z^\delta_L)^2=\delta\sum_{r=1}^L1=L\delta$. ◻

</details>

The degree limit has identified the scalar law $Z^\delta_L$ and the finite-tree edge limit in <a href="#eq:eams-corrected-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-corrected-value">[eq:eams-corrected-value]</a>. We next compare these two quantities with the Parisi diffusion at the fixed temperature. The symmetric-power argument in the following subsection will then realize the same finite formula as a rooted vector, so that its energy can be used in the clipping and synthesis steps. The normalized Euler and martingale comparison below is related to <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[AMS2020](#ref-AMS2020), Proposition 5.3]. We give the estimates for the present fixed-temperature tree construction.

<a id="stmt-lem-eams-positive-continuum"></a>
<div id="lem:eams-positive-continuum" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:euler-normalization-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:martingale-euler-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:value-riemann-bound"></a>
**Lemma 38** (The continuum limit at fixed temperature). *Fix $\beta_0>1$ and $0<T<q$, and let $L=\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor$. There is a coupling with the diffusion <a href="#eq:positive-parisi-diffusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-parisi-diffusion">[eq:positive-parisi-diffusion]</a> and a constant $C=C(\beta_0,T)$ such that, for all sufficiently small $\delta$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \max_{r\delta\le T}\mathbb E|X^\delta_r-X_{r\delta}|^2&\le C\delta,
 &\max_{r\delta\le T}|c_{\delta,r}-1|&\le C\sqrt\delta,
 \label{eq:euler-normalization-bound}\\
 \mathbb E|Z^\delta_L-m(T,X_T)|^2&\le C\delta,
 \label{eq:martingale-euler-bound}\\
 \left|\delta\sum_{r=1}^{L-1}c_{\delta,r}\mathbb Ea(t_r,X^\delta_r)
                    -\int_0^T\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt\right|
    &\le C\sqrt\delta.
 \label{eq:value-riemann-bound}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-eams-positive-continuum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-eams-positive-continuum</summary>

*Proof.* Take $U_0$ independent of $W$ and $\sqrt\delta\,U_r=W_{r\delta}-W_{(r-1)\delta}$ for $r\ge1$. Let $\tau_\delta(t)=\delta\lfloor t/\delta\rfloor$ and use the continuous Euler interpolation $$\overline X^\delta_t
 =\sqrt\delta\,U_0+
       \int_0^tb(\tau_\delta(s),X^\delta_{\lfloor s/\delta\rfloor})\,\mathrm ds
       +W_t.$$ The drift is bounded and Lipschitz in space and time on $[0,T]$. Hence $\mathbb E|\overline X^\delta_s-X^\delta_{\lfloor s/\delta\rfloor}|^2
\le C\delta$. Subtracting the integral equations for $X$ and $\overline X^\delta$, using Cauchy–Schwarz, and then Gronwall gives $$\mathbb E\sup_{s\le T}|\overline X^\delta_s-X_s|^2\le C\delta.$$ The initial contribution is $\mathbb E|\sqrt\delta U_0|^2=\delta$. This proves the first bound of <a href="#eq:euler-normalization-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler-normalization-bound">[eq:euler-normalization-bound]</a>.

Because $a$ is bounded and spatially Lipschitz, and $\mathbb Ea(t_r,X_{t_r})^2=1$, $$\max_{r\delta\le T}
 \left|\mathbb Ea(t_r,X^\delta_r)^2-1\right|\le C\sqrt\delta.$$ For small $\delta$ all these expectations lie in $[1/2,3/2]$. The map $x\mapsto x^{-1/2}$ is Lipschitz on that interval, proving the second bound of <a href="#eq:euler-normalization-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler-normalization-bound">[eq:euler-normalization-bound]</a>.

Equation <a href="#eq:eams-limit-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eams-limit-output">[eq:eams-limit-output]</a> is the stochastic integral $$Z^\delta_L=\int_0^{L\delta}
 c_{\delta,\lfloor t/\delta\rfloor}
 a(\tau_\delta(t),X^\delta_{\lfloor t/\delta\rfloor})\,\mathrm dW_t.$$ The integrand differs from $a(t,X_t)$ in squared mean by at most $C\delta$, uniformly in $t\le L\delta$. This follows from <a href="#eq:euler-normalization-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler-normalization-bound">[eq:euler-normalization-bound]</a>, the spatial and temporal Lipschitz bounds for $a$, and $\mathbb E|X_t-X_{\tau_\delta(t)}|^2\le C\delta$. Itô isometry, including the last interval of length at most $\delta$, gives <a href="#eq:martingale-euler-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:martingale-euler-bound">[eq:martingale-euler-bound]</a>, since $m(T,X_T)=\int_0^Ta(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dW_t$. The same estimates in first mean compare the sum in <a href="#eq:value-riemann-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:value-riemann-bound">[eq:value-riemann-bound]</a> with its Riemann integral. The omitted first and last intervals have total length at most $3\delta$ and bounded integrands. This proves the final estimate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Strong convergence of classical descendant formulas

We next place this classical construction in the Hilbert space used by Proposition <a href="#prop:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:energy">4</a>. This step requires norm convergence, not only a CLT in distribution.

We use the Gaussian lift $\mathsf G$ of Section <a href="#subsec:classical-gaussian-sector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:classical-gaussian-sector">2.3</a> and the finite descendant formulas of Definition <a href="#def:finite-descendant-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:finite-descendant-formula">6</a>. In the directed EAMS calculation the sent columns are $A^{r-1}u^r$, with $u^0=g$ for the first propagation. The node output uses these same columns. Replacing $D+1$ root children by $D$, or the final normalization $\sqrt{D+1}$ by $\sqrt D$, does not change the limiting formula.

<a id="stmt-lem-classical-strong-limit"></a>
<div id="lem:classical-strong-limit" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:classical-energy-transfer"></a>
**Lemma 39** (The symmetric-power realization). *Consider a fixed finite descendant formula whose pointwise functions are smooth with all derivatives of polynomial growth. Assume its leaf seeds have moments of every order and every aggregated child column has mean zero under its finite-degree child law for each $D$. Its finite-degree root vector converges in norm, under the symmetric occupation embeddings, to the formula obtained by replacing every normalized sum by $\mathsf G$. The same conclusion holds for a convergent finite collection of deterministic coefficients in the formula. The finite-degree root vectors have a uniformly bounded first child-occupation moment. Consequently, for centered equivariant outputs $f_D$ and their limit $f$, $$\lim_{D\to\infty}\frac{D+1}{2\sqrt D}\mathbb Ef_D(o)f_D(v)
       =\mathcal E(f),\qquad o\sim v.
 \label{eq:classical-energy-transfer}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-classical-strong-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-classical-strong-limit</summary>

*Proof.* The proof first treats characteristic vectors, then arbitrary pointwise maps, and finally the occupation bound needed for energy. We use induction on formula depth. All intermediate moments are uniformly bounded: normalized centered sums satisfy the moment bound used in Lemma <a href="#lem:eams-finite-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:eams-finite-step">37</a>, and polynomial-growth functions preserve the existence of sufficiently high uniform moments.

Suppose the real centered child columns $f_{D,1},\ldots,f_{D,k}$ already converge in norm to $f_1,\ldots,f_k$ in the common child Hilbert space. Fix $t\in\mathbb R^k$ and put $F_D=\sum_jt_jf_{D,j}$, $F=\sum_jt_jf_j$. The limits are centered because the embeddings preserve the vacuum and the convergence is in norm. A joint characteristic vector for the parent sums is the symmetric tensor power of $$h_D=\exp(\mathrm iF_D/\sqrt D)\boldsymbol\Omega
          =\alpha_D\boldsymbol\Omega+\zeta_D,
 \qquad \zeta_D\perp\boldsymbol\Omega.$$ Taylor expansion in $L^2$, justified by the uniform fourth moment, and centering give $$\alpha_D=1-\frac{\lVert F_D\rVert^2}{2D}+O(D^{-3/2}),
 \qquad \sqrt D\,\zeta_D\longrightarrow\mathrm iF.$$ The $j$-particle component of the embedded tensor power is $$\binom Dj^{1/2}\alpha_D^{D-j}\zeta_D^{\otimes j}
 \longrightarrow
 e^{-\lVert F\rVert^2/2}\frac{(\mathrm iF)^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{j!}}.$$ These are the components of $e^{\mathrm iq(F)}\boldsymbol\Omega$. The expected occupation of the left-hand vector is $D\lVert \zeta_D\rVert^2=O(1)$, so its occupation tails are uniformly small. Thus convergence of each fixed grade implies norm convergence of the complete vector. A root with $M=D+1$ children has the same proof: use $M$ factors and the normalization $M^{-1/2}$, with the same convergent child columns. Alternatively, retaining $D^{-1/2}$ at that root gives the same limit because $M/D\to1$. The occupation bound in either convention is $M\lVert \zeta_D\rVert^2=O(1)$.

Finite linear combinations of these exponentials, multiplied by bounded measurable functions of the independent root seed, are dense in the limiting $L^2$ space. Indeed the root seed and aggregate fields have a product law. Products of dense families in the two factors are dense, and exponentials are dense in the field factor: an orthogonal $L^2$ vector would give a finite signed measure with zero Fourier transform and would therefore vanish. There is a useful precision concerning measurable seed factors. Let $S$ denote the root seed, $Y_D$ the finite list of aggregate fields, and $Y$ its limit. The law of $S$ is unchanged with $D$ and is independent of $Y_D$ and $Y$. For $P(s,y)=\sum_jg_j(s)e^{\mathrm it_j\cdot y}$ with bounded measurable $g_j$, and a bounded target $F(s,y)$ continuous in $y$ for every fixed $s$, the function $|F(s,y)-P(s,y)|^2$ is bounded continuous in $y$ for each fixed $s$. Weak convergence of $Y_D$ therefore gives convergence of its field integral pointwise in $s$. The bound is uniform in $s$, so dominated convergence in the unchanged root-seed law proves $$\mathbb E|F(S,Y_D)-P(S,Y_D)|^2
       \longrightarrow\mathbb E|F(S,Y)-P(S,Y)|^2.$$ Thus measurability of $g_j$ does not require a discontinuous test function in an appeal to joint weak convergence. For a polynomial-growth target, first multiply by smooth cutoffs outside large boxes in the seed and field variables.
